### 1. **`sh~` is a typo**
Editors often save backups as `something.sh~`. There is only:

(no `~` at the end). If you run `…sh~`, the shell can’t find the file and you get odd errors.

**Use exactly:**

```bash
bash scripts/setup_notebook_venv.sh
```

(from `/home/fadia/Documents/vlst`), **or**:

```bash
bash /home/fadia/Documents/vlst/scripts/setup_notebook_venv.sh
```

Do **not** use `./Cursor-2.6.19-x86_64.AppImage` to run this script.

---

### 2. **Why `Cursor-2.6.19-x86_64.AppImage` appears**
Inside Cursor, `python3` on your `PATH` can end up tied to the AppImage. Creating a venv with that “Python” breaks `pip`/shebangs and you see errors mentioning the AppImage.

The script is updated to:

- Prefer **`/usr/bin/python3`** when you don’t set anything else.
- Refuse to continue if the chosen interpreter is an **`.AppImage`**.
- Stop with a clear message if **`.venv`** was already created with that broken setup.

If it tells you to remove `.venv`, run:

```bash
cd /home/fadia/Documents/vlst
rm -rf .venv
PYTHON=/usr/bin/python3 bash scripts/setup_notebook_venv.sh
```

That forces the distro Python even if `PATH` is wrong in Cursor’s terminal.

---

**Summary:** Run the real script name (no `~`), with `bash`, and use `PYTHON=/usr/bin/python3` if Cursor’s terminal still confuses Python.

Lines like:

are **not** a command to copy. So: **yes, it can show up when you use Cursor**, if the integrated environment puts a **`python3`/`python` that is really the AppImage** (or a shim) ahead of the real system Python.

1. **Run the script with `bash`, not the AppImage**

   ```bash
   cd /home/fadia/Documents/vlst
   bash scripts/setup_notebook_venv.sh
   ```

   Name must be **`setup_notebook_venv.sh`** — **not** `setup_notebook_venv.sh~`.

2. **Or use the task we added (no PATH tricks needed in your head)**  
   In Cursor: **Terminal → Run Task… → “Setup notebook .venv (TabPFN / Jupyter kernel)”**  
   That runs the script with **`PYTHON=/usr/bin/python3`**.

3. **Workspace settings**  
   This repo’s **`.vscode/settings.json`** now sets **`terminal.integrated.env.linux`** so **`PATH`** starts with **`/usr/bin`** and **`PYTHON=/usr/bin/python3`**, so **new integrated terminals** are much less likely to pick Cursor’s AppImage as “Python.”  
   If you rely on **conda/pyenv** being first on `PATH`, you can remove or adjust that block.

4. **Optional**  
   One-time setup in **Konsole / an external terminal** with plain Manjaro `python3` also avoids Cursor’s terminal quirks entirely.

**Bottom line:** Keep using Cursor; don’t launch the AppImage as your shell command. Use **`bash scripts/setup_notebook_venv.sh`**, avoid **`sh~`**, and use the **Run Task** or **`PYTHON=/usr/bin/python3`** if the integrated terminal still mis-detects Python.

### ASSISTANT
### Two different **filenames**

| Name | What it is |
|------|------------|
| **`setup_notebook_venv.sh`** | The real script in the repo: `scripts/setup_notebook_venv.sh`. This is what you should run. |
| **`setup_notebook_venv.sh~`** | A **different** name. The `~` is **not** “home” here — it’s **part of the filename**. Many editors (especially **Emacs**) save a **backup** next to the file with a **`~`** suffix, e.g. `foo.sh~`. That file may be old, missing, or not tracked in git. |

So they are not “the same script with a variant spelling”; they are **two possible paths**, and only the first one is guaranteed to be the one we mean.

### Why saying “not `…sh~`” is helpful

- If you run `bash scripts/setup_notebook_venv.sh~` and **no backup was ever created**, the shell correctly says **No such file or directory**.
- Even if **`…sh~` exists**, it’s often a **stale copy** from an old save — not what you want for setup.

### Rule of thumb

Use the name **without** the trailing **`~`**:  
`scripts/setup_notebook_venv.sh`

### USER
<user_query>
what is cross validation and what is stratified crossvalidation?
</user_query>

### ASSISTANT
### Cross-validation (CV)

**Cross-validation** is a way to estimate how well a model **generalizes** when you don’t have a separate held-out test set you want to touch only once, or when you want a **more stable** performance estimate than a single train/test split.

You split the data into **K folds** (e.g. 5). For each fold **k**:

1. Train the model on **all folds except k**  
2. Evaluate on **fold k** (the “validation” part)

You average (or summarize) the K scores. That gives you a sense of performance **across different partitions** of the same data, which reduces luck from one random split.

Common variant: **K-fold CV** — divide data into K roughly equal parts; each part is used once as validation.

---

### Stratified cross-validation

**Stratified** CV is the same K-fold idea, but when you build each fold you **preserve the proportion of each class** (e.g. positive/negative in binary classification) in **every** fold as much as possible.

So if your dataset is **imbalanced** (e.g. few positives), stratification avoids a fold that accidentally has **almost no positives**, which would make scores noisy or meaningless.

In scikit-learn this is **`StratifiedKFold`** for classification; for regression, “stratified” usually means binning the target or other schemes, not the default `StratifiedKFold`.

---

### One-line contrast

- **CV**: reuse data in K structured train/val rounds for a robust metric.  
- **Stratified CV**: same, but **each fold keeps similar class balance** — especially important for **classification with imbalance**.

<user_query>
here are my results what does it mean? why f1 and precision are so low while pr-auc is high?
Confusion matrix:
 [[971  48]
 [  1  17]]
accuracy: 0.9527
f1: 0.4096
recall: 0.9444
precision: 0.2615
roc_auc: 0.9942
pr_auc: 0.8423
</user_query>

### ASSISTANT
Here’s how to read **your** numbers.

### What the confusion matrix says

Sklearn uses **rows = true class**, **columns = predicted class** (for classes `0` and `1`):

| | Pred 0 | Pred 1 |
|--|--------|--------|
| **True 0** | 971 | 48 |
| **True 1** | 1 | 17 |

So:

- **True class 1** (the rare “positive”) appears only **18** times (`1 + 17`).
- **True class 0** appears **1019** times — the problem is **very imbalanced** (~98% vs ~2%).

### Why accuracy is high but precision is low

- **Accuracy 0.95** = mostly “predict 0 and be right,” because class 0 dominates.
- **Recall 0.94** on the positive class = of the **18** real positives, you found **17** (only **1** missed). So the model is **aggressive about calling positive**.
- **Precision 0.26** = when it says positive, it’s often wrong: **17** true positives vs **48** false positives among positive predictions → \(17 / (17+48) \approx 0.26\).

So: **you catch almost all positives, but you also fire “positive” on many negatives**, which crushes precision and pulls **F1** down (F1 is the harmonic mean of precision and recall).

### Why PR-AUC can look “high” while precision is low

- **PR-AUC** (average precision over the precision–recall curve) measures **how well predicted scores rank positives above negatives**, averaged across thresholds — not how good you are at **one** fixed threshold.
- You can have **good ranking** (high PR-AUC) but, at the **default 0.5 threshold**, still **many false positives** → **low precision** and **moderate F1**.

**ROC-AUC 0.99** is also easy to inflate when negatives are abundant: separating “negative vs everyone” can look excellent even while **positive precision** stays modest.

### Practical takeaway

Your model is **strong at ordering** examples (scores) and **finds most true positives**, but its **operating point** (likely threshold 0.5) is **too trigger-happy** for the positive class. Improving **precision** usually means **raising the threshold** (or using a cost-sensitive rule), accepting some **recall** loss — or using **class weights / probability calibration** if TabPFN exposes that.

If you want, paste how you call `predict` / whether you use `balance_probabilities` or a custom threshold and we can tie it to the next tuning step.

### ASSISTANT
Here’s what was changed in `tabpfn.ipynb` and why.

### 1. **Threshold tuning (your precision / F1 issue)**  
- In **§2** added:
  - **`DECISION_THRESHOLD`**: `None` (default) = search a grid of thresholds on **P(class = 1)**.
  - **`THRESHOLD_VAL_FRACTION = 0.15`**: stratified **train → fit / val** split only for choosing the threshold.
- **§5** now:
  - If **`DECISION_THRESHOLD is None`**: fit a temporary model on **85%** of train, choose the threshold that **maximizes F1** on the **15%** val slice, then **refit on full `X_train`** and apply that threshold on the test set:  
    `y_pred = (y_prob >= HOLDOUT_THRESHOLD).astype(int)`.
  - If you set e.g. **`DECISION_THRESHOLD = 0.5`**, behavior matches the old **`clf.predict()`**-style rule.
- Metrics and **`tabpfn_holdout_metrics.csv`** now include **`decision_threshold`**. **`tabpfn_run_meta.joblib`** stores **`decision_threshold`** and **`balance_probabilities`**.
- **§6 (CV)** uses the same **`HOLDOUT_THRESHOLD`** as the hold-out block (`globals().get("HOLDOUT_THRESHOLD", 0.5)` if you run CV without §5).

**§5 markdown** briefly documents this and points at **`BALANCE_PROBABILITIES`** (still **`True`** in your config).

### 2. **`predict` vs `balance_probabilities`**  
- **`BALANCE_PROBABILITIES`** is only passed into **`TabPFNClassifier(...)`** (unchanged in §2).  
- **`predict` / threshold**: previously **`y_pred = clf.predict(X_test)`** (default cutoff). Now explicit **`y_prob`** + **`HOLDOUT_THRESHOLD`** when tuning is enabled.

### 3. **Security: API key removed from the notebook**  
The notebook had a **hard-coded JWT** in a cell. That cell now **only checks** that **`TABPFN_TOKEN`** is set and tells you to use **`export TABPFN_TOKEN=...`** or a **local-only** cell.  
**If that key was ever committed or shared, rotate it in the PriorLabs account.**

### What you should do  
1. **`export TABPFN_TOKEN='...'`** (or set it in a **local** cell **without committing**).  
2. Run the **token check** cell, then **§5**. With **`DECISION_THRESHOLD = None`**, you should see **higher precision / F1** if the model ranks positives well (at some cost to recall vs 0.5).  
3. To compare to the old behavior: set **`DECISION_THRESHOLD = 0.5`** in §2 and rerun.

Here is where things stand after the handoff fixes and the small edits just made.

### What was wrong in §5 (your report and confusion matrix)

The matrix `[[1250, 0], [23, 0]]` means **every row was predicted class 0**. That is not “math broken”; it is **the model (or the pipeline) never predicting 1**. In your earlier run the usual causes were:

1. **Silent failure → all zeros** — if the API was not usable (e.g. missing `OPENAI_API_KEY`) and the code fell back to `0` on errors, you get exactly this pattern.
2. **Wrong subsample split** — if `train_test_split` was unpacked backwards, you were not on the intended small‑N subset (now fixed to `idx, _ = ...`).

After the fixes, the notebook **fails fast** without a key and **does not** default failed parses to 0, so you should see real failures instead of a fake “perfect” majority class.

### ROC-AUC and PR-AUC

They **are** defined in the metrics cell when both classes appear in `y_test` and `y_prob` is not constant:

- **`roc_auc_score(y_test, y_prob)`** — ROC-AUC from **`p_event`**.
- **`average_precision_score(y_test, y_prob)`** — this is **PR-AUC** (average precision over the precision–recall curve).

They are stored in `llm_small_n_meta.json` as `roc_auc` and `pr_auc`, and the CSV has **`p_event`** for threshold tuning. `classification_report` uses **`zero_division=0`** so the sklearn precision warning is suppressed.

### What I changed just now

1. **§5 markdown** — Clarifies that the report uses hard labels from JSON `label`, while ROC-AUC / PR-AUC use **`p_event`**, and that the CSV includes **`p_event`**.
2. **Removed** the empty trailing code cell (`dcc86ff4`) so the notebook ends cleanly.

### ASSISTANT
The notebook reads **`os.environ`** (`LLM_BACKEND`, `OPENAI_API_KEY`, etc.). Whatever process starts the **Jupyter kernel** must have those variables set.

### Option A — OpenAI (this shell session)

In the **same terminal** where you start Jupyter (or before you open Cursor from that terminal):

```bash
export OPENAI_API_KEY='sk-...your-key...'
```

Optional model override:

```bash
export OPENAI_MODEL='gpt-4o-mini'
```

Then start the notebook kernel from that environment (or restart Cursor from that terminal so child processes inherit it).

### Option B — Ollama (no OpenAI key)

1. Install and run [Ollama](https://ollama.com/), pull a model, e.g. `ollama pull llama3`.
2. In the shell used for the kernel:

```bash
export LLM_BACKEND=ollama
export OLLAMA_MODEL='llama3'   # optional; default in notebook is llama3
```

Default URL is `http://127.0.0.1:11434/api/chat` unless you set `OLLAMA_URL`.

### If the notebook still does not see the variable

- **Cursor / VS Code**: Integrated terminals often load your shell config; the **notebook kernel** may have been started earlier or from a different environment. **Restart the kernel** after exporting, or start Cursor from a terminal where you already ran `export ...`.
- **One-off run** (parent process gets the vars):

```bash
cd /home/fadia/Documents/vlst
export OPENAI_API_KEY='sk-...'
# or: export LLM_BACKEND=ollama
.venv/bin/python -m jupyter notebook code/modeling/llm_tabular_small_n.ipynb
```

### Persistence (optional)

Put `export OPENAI_API_KEY=...` in `~/.zshrc` only if you accept the usual risk of keeping secrets in a dotfile; many people prefer a password manager + session-only `export`, or a tool like **direnv** with a gitignored `.envrc`.

Do **not** commit the key into the repo or into a committed notebook cell.

### First step to “have a key”

You get an **OpenAI API key** from OpenAI’s site after you have an account. The first step is **sign up / log in** at [https://platform.openai.com/](https://platform.openai.com/).

Then:

1. Open **API keys**: [https://platform.openai.com/api-keys](https://platform.openai.com/api-keys) (from the dashboard: your account → **API keys**).
2. Click **Create new secret key**, name it (e.g. “vlst-notebook”), create it.
3. **Copy it once** and store it somewhere safe (password manager). OpenAI usually **does not show it again**.

After that, in your shell (before running the notebook):

```bash
export OPENAI_API_KEY='sk-...paste the key...'
```

**Important:** OpenAI’s API is **paid** (usage-based). You need **billing enabled** and usually a small credit balance; check **Billing** / **Usage** on the same platform site. If you skip billing, calls will fail even with a valid-looking key.

---

### If you do not want an OpenAI key

Use **Ollama** locally (no OpenAI account):

1. Install from [https://ollama.com/](https://ollama.com/), run the app/daemon.
2. `ollama pull llama3` (or another model).
3. In your shell:

```bash
export LLM_BACKEND=ollama
```

Then run the notebook; it talks to `http://127.0.0.1:11434` by default.

So: **first step for an OpenAI key** = create a **platform.openai.com** account → **API keys** → **Create new secret key** → enable **billing** if required. **First step without any key** = use **Ollama** instead.

### ASSISTANT

### ASSISTANT
Your numbers already look strong for a **very rare** class (18 positives in the holdout): ROC-AUC **0.99** and PR-AUC **0.84** say the **ranking** is excellent. What limits **precision** and **F1** is mostly **where you cut** the score and **how noisy a single split is** with so few events.

### Why precision is “only” ~0.52

From the confusion matrix you have **14 true positives** and **13 false positives**. Precision for class 1 is  
\(\text{TP} / (\text{TP} + \text{FP}) = 14 / 27 \approx 0.52\).  
So the model is **slightly too eager** to call “1” at the tuned threshold: good recall (14/18), but many alarms on negatives.

### Ideas that actually move precision / F1

**1. Change what you optimize on the validation slice (biggest lever)**  
You currently pick the threshold to **maximize F1** on 15% of train. That explicitly **balances** precision and recall, so it will not maximize precision.

- **Higher precision**: tune the threshold to maximize **F-beta with \(\beta < 1\)** (e.g. **F0.5** weights precision more than recall), or maximize **precision subject to recall ≥ r** (e.g. recall ≥ 0.7).
- **Higher F1 on test**: validation F1 can be **lucky** with ~tens of positives; the chosen threshold (0.866) can be a bit off on the test set. **Repeated stratified CV** (or several val seeds) to pick the threshold usually stabilizes this.

**2. Move the threshold along the PR curve (conceptual)**  
ROC-AUC is almost saturated; for imbalance, **PR-AUC** is more informative. Your operating point is a **single threshold**. To raise precision, you **raise the threshold** (accept fewer predicted positives). That almost always **lowers recall** unless scores are perfectly separated—so “raise F1 and precision together” is only possible if the model can **separate** better or you **change the objective** (see above).

**3. Try `balance_probabilities=False` (A/B, not guaranteed)**  
You use `balance_probabilities=True`, which can help calibration in some setups but also **changes** probability scale and optimal threshold. It is worth one **controlled comparison** on the same CV with the same threshold search.

**4. Inputs TabPFN sees**  
Your notebook already notes **`USE_RAW_FEATURES`** vs the same processed matrices as other models. Worth an experiment: sometimes raw-ish tabular inputs behave differently for TabPFN, which can shift the FP/FN trade-off.

**5. Accept variance from tiny test sets**  
With **18** positives, changing **one** prediction moves recall by ~5.6% and can move F1 noticeably. Reporting **bootstrap CI** on the test set (or CV mean ± std) avoids over-interpreting a single split.

**6. If you need both high precision and high recall**  
That usually needs **better features**, **more labeled data**, or a **second stage** (e.g. TabPFN as ranker + cheap confirmatory rule/clinic variables, or ensemble with a sparse linear model that suppresses some FPs). TabPFN alone cannot always bend the ROC/PR curve further without new signal.

### Practical takeaway

- To **raise precision** with minimal code change: **raise the decision threshold** or retune using **F0.5** / **precision@minimum recall** on the stratified val slice instead of pure F1.  
- To **raise F1** without sacrificing what you care about: **stabilize threshold choice** (repeated CV / multiple seeds) and optionally **redefine the tuning metric** to match the clinical cost (false alarm vs miss).

If you want this baked into `tabpfn.ipynb`, say whether you prefer **F0.5**, **precision at fixed recall**, or a **small grid of thresholds** printed with precision/recall/F1 so you can pick a point on the curve manually.

tell me what is balance_probabilities and calibration? what do they do?
</user_query>

### ASSISTANT
[REDACTED]

### ASSISTANT
[REDACTED]

### ASSISTANT
Here is a clean distinction between the two.

### `balance_probabilities` (TabPFN-specific)

In `TabPFNClassifier`, this is a **post-processing step on the scores that come out of the model**, applied when you call `predict_proba()` (and related paths): after the ensemble is combined (and after settings like softmax temperature), TabPFN can **reshape the class probabilities using the class counts from your training set**.

Intuitively: the raw network output may be **pulled toward the majority class** when labels are very imbalanced. Turning this on **pushes probability mass toward the rare class** in a way that depends on how many 0s vs 1s you had in `fit()`.

From the library’s own docstring (paraphrased): it can help on **highly imbalanced** problems when you care about behavior that treats classes more even-handedly (examples they give include **balanced accuracy**, **balanced log loss**, **macro ROC-AUC**, and similar).

**What it is not:** It is **not** a guarantee that “30% means 30% of positives in the real world.” It is **prior / count-based rebalancing** of the model’s probabilities, not full statistical calibration.

**Practical effect:** It changes **both** the reported probabilities and any **threshold** you tune on top of them (so optimal threshold with `True` vs `False` can shift a lot).

---

### Calibration (general ML meaning)

**Calibration** is about whether **predicted probabilities match reality** in a frequency sense.

- If the model outputs **0.3** for “probability of class 1” for many patients, and you group all those patients, you want roughly **30%** of them to truly be class 1 (after enough data).  
- A model can have **excellent ROC-AUC** (great **ranking**) but **poor calibration** (probabilities are systematically too small or too large).  
- A model can be **calibrated** but still useless if it barely separates the classes (AUC could be weak).

Common **calibration methods** (outside TabPFN) include **Platt scaling**, **isotonic regression**, and **temperature scaling**, usually fit on a **validation** set so they do not steal information from the test set.

---

### Short comparison

| | **`balance_probabilities`** | **Calibration** |
|---|-----------------------------|------------------|
| **What it does** | Adjusts TabPFN’s `predict_proba` using **training class frequencies** (built-in step). | Aligns **P(y\|x)** with **observed frequencies** (often a separate step or model). |
| **Goal** | Mitigate **class imbalance** effects on probabilities. | Make **numbers mean what they say** in a frequentist sense. |
| **Typical use** | Imbalanced tabular classification with TabPFN. | Decision-making, risk reporting, combining models, fair comparison of probabilities. |

So: **`balance_probabilities`** is a **TabPFN option for imbalance-aware probability adjustment**. **Calibration** is the broader idea that **probabilities should match long-run outcomes**, usually checked with reliability diagrams / Brier-related views and sometimes fixed with a second calibration stage.

§5 and §6 now save metrics, meta, ROC/PR plot, and CV summary **under those tagged names** and print the full paths. `tabpfn_run_meta.joblib` also stores `"result_tag"`.

### How to compare `balance_probabilities` True vs False

### Before this change

Runs **did** save outputs under `data/result/modeling_tabpfn/`, but **always the same filenames**, so **each new run overwrote** the last one (`tabpfn_holdout_metrics.csv`, `tabpfn_run_meta.joblib`, `tabpfn_roc_pr.png`, and `tabpfn_cv_summary.csv` when CV is enabled). There was **no automatic history** per run.

### What changed in the notebook

§2 now sets a **`TABPFN_RESULT_TAG`** (default: **UTC timestamp** + **`_balTrue` / `_balFalse`**) and a helper **`tabpfn_result_path(...)`**. §5 and §6 write **tagged** files and print **`Wrote ...`** paths. `tabpfn_run_meta.joblib` also stores `"result_tag"`.

You can force the tag with:

`export TABPFN_RESULT_TAG=my_experiment_false`  
(If you reuse the **same** tag for two runs, they will **overwrite** each other again.)

### How to test with vs without `balance_probabilities`

1. Run **§2** with `BALANCE_PROBABILITIES = True`, then **§5** (and **§6** if you use CV).  
2. Set `BALANCE_PROBABILITIES = False`, **run §2 again** (so the tag reflects the new setting and a new time if you use the default), then **§5** / **§6** again.  
3. Compare the two sets of `tabpfn_holdout_metrics_*.csv` (and plots) in `data/result/modeling_tabpfn/`.

**Important:** `HOLDOLD_THRESHOLD` is tuned on the val slice when `DECISION_THRESHOLD is None`, so each setting gets its own threshold; that is what you want for a fair comparison.

### ASSISTANT
Checking your TabPFN (§8b) settings and training data scale.

[REDACTED]

### ASSISTANT
Yes — **it can be “normal” in the sense that nothing is necessarily broken**, but it is **not** something you should treat as a typical quick model fit.

In your notebook, §8b is doing roughly this:

- **`TabPFNClassifier.fit(X_merge, y_merge)`** on the **merged** training slice (often **thousands of rows** after your pipeline, and **more rows if SMOTE ran**).
- **`TABPFN_N_ESTIMATORS = 8`** → TabPFN is effectively doing **eight** heavy passes / sub-models, not one.
- **`TABPFN_IGNORE_PRETRAINING_LIMITS = True`** → you are explicitly allowing **larger-than–pre-training-range** settings, which TabPFN can handle but often at **very high** time cost.
- **`TABPFN_DEVICE = "auto"`** → if there is **no usable GPU**, `"auto"` often ends up on **CPU**, where TabPFN can run for **many hours** on ~4k+ rows × ~170 features with multiple estimators.

So **200+ minutes with no finish** is **plausible** on CPU with those settings, especially with SMOTE-inflated `X_merge` and `n_estimators=8`.

**Practical knobs (fastest wins first):**

1. Set **`TABPFN_N_ESTIMATORS = 1`** (or 2) for a sanity run.  
2. Force **`TABPFN_DEVICE = "cuda"`** only if you actually have a CUDA GPU; otherwise you are on CPU.  
3. Temporarily set **`RUN_TABPFN_BLEND = False`** when you do not need §9 four-way TabPFN blends.  
4. If the API supports it for your TabPFN version, try **`ignore_pretraining_limits=False`** on a **smaller subsample** first to see runtime (full data may exceed limits — that is why the flag exists).

**Bottom line:** long wall-clock time here usually means **compute load**, not Jupyter “being stuck,” unless the kernel is truly frozen (no CPU usage). Check **CPU/GPU utilization** in another terminal (`nvidia-smi` / `htop`); if one core is pegged for hours, it is still working, just slowly.

### ASSISTANT
Checking the system for an NVIDIA GPU and CUDA availability:

[REDACTED]

### ASSISTANT
[REDACTED]

### ASSISTANT
Here is a simple way to tell on **Linux** (run these in a normal terminal on **your** machine, not only inside a restricted sandbox).

### 1. **Do you have an NVIDIA GPU?**

```bash
lspci | grep -i nvidia
```

- **No output** → usually **no NVIDIA discrete GPU** (or it is disabled / not visible).  
- **Lines mentioning NVIDIA** → you likely have hardware; you still need a driver (next step).

On many laptops you only see **Intel** / **AMD** graphics, like:

`VGA compatible controller: Intel Corporation ...`

That is **not** NVIDIA **CUDA** (CUDA here means **NVIDIA’s** stack).

### 2. **Is the NVIDIA driver installed and working?**

```bash
nvidia-smi
```

- **Table with GPU name, driver version, memory** → you have a working **NVIDIA + driver** setup; CUDA userland is usually usable from apps that support it.  
- **`command not found`** → driver/tools not installed **or** no NVIDIA GPU.  
- **Error about driver / device** → hardware or driver problem.

### 3. **Does PyTorch (in your venv) see CUDA?**

From the repo, with your notebook venv activated:

```bash
.venv/bin/python -c "import torch; print(torch.cuda.is_available()); print(torch.cuda.get_device_name(0) if torch.cuda.is_available() else 'no cuda')"
```

- **`True`** and a device name → PyTorch can use **CUDA** for that install.  
- **`False`** or `No module named 'torch'` → either **CPU-only** PyTorch, no GPU driver, or torch not installed in that env.

**How to read this for TabPFN:** `TABPFN_DEVICE = "cuda"` only helps if you have **NVIDIA + working CUDA** in the stack TabPFN uses (often that means a GPU build of PyTorch / the library’s CUDA path). Integrated **Intel** graphics is **not** NVIDIA CUDA.

If you paste the output of `lspci | grep -iE 'nvidia|vga|3d'` and `nvidia-smi` from **your** machine, we can interpret it line by line.

### USER
<user_query>
which one is better for this task?
T4 GPU or v5e-1 TPU?
</user_query>

### ASSISTANT
For **your** stack (CatBoost / XGBoost / LightGBM, optional **TabPFN**, `sklearn`, notebooks on a single training matrix), **a T4 (or any NVIDIA GPU with CUDA) is the better fit** than a **v5e-1 TPU**.

**Why T4 fits this task**

- **TabPFN** in practice is built around **PyTorch** and “normal” GPU/CPU paths; **CUDA** is what people actually use and debug. Getting TabPFN to run well on **TPU** is non-default and often not worth it.
- **XGBoost / LightGBM / CatBoost** are **CUDA-oriented** (or CPU), not **TPU-first**. You do not get a turnkey speedup on TPU for this notebook-shaped code.
- A **T4** is a straightforward drop-in for **GPU-enabled** PyTorch and many tree libraries on a **single VM**.

**When v5e-1 TPU is “better”**

- Huge **JAX / TensorFlow** training jobs written **for TPU** (large batches, `pjit` / SPMD, etc.).
- You are **not** relying on the usual sklearn + PyTorch tabular notebook path.

**Bottom line:** for this VLST modeling notebook, choose **T4 (CUDA)** unless you have already committed to rewriting the pipeline for **JAX on TPU** and confirmed TabPFN (or a replacement) supports that path end-to-end—which is usually **no** for this kind of project.

### USER
<user_query>
how can I activate GPU? how can i force notebook to use GPU in the google collab?
</user_query>

### ASSISTANT
## Turn on a GPU in Google Colab (host side)

1. **Runtime → Change runtime type** (or **Runtime → Disconnect and delete runtime** if you already picked CPU and want a clean switch).
2. Under **Hardware accelerator**, choose **GPU** (e.g. T4). **Save**.
3. Re-run from the top (or at least re-import libraries).

Optional: **Runtime → Manage sessions** shows what you are attached to.

---

## Check that the runtime actually sees a GPU

In a Colab code cell:

```python
!nvidia-smi
```

You should see an NVIDIA GPU name and driver info. If it says the command is missing or errors, you are not on a GPU runtime.

If you use PyTorch:

```python
import torch
print(torch.cuda.is_available())
print(torch.cuda.get_device_name(0) if torch.cuda.is_available() else "CPU only")
```

---

## “Force” your notebook to use the GPU (code side)

Colab does **not** auto-move every library to the GPU. You set **device** where the library supports it.

**TabPFN** (matches your `advanced.ipynb` §8b style):

```python
TABPFN_DEVICE = "cuda"   # not "auto"
```

and when constructing the classifier:

```python
TabPFNClassifier(..., device="cuda", ...)
```

**PyTorch** in general: put model and tensors on `cuda` (or `device = torch.device("cuda")`).

**XGBoost / LightGBM / CatBoost**: each has its own GPU flags (e.g. XGBoost `device="cuda"` in recent versions, CatBoost `task_type="GPU"`, LightGBM `device="gpu"`). Your notebook must already use those if you want GPU there; changing Colab runtime alone is not enough.

---

## Practical notes for Colab

- **Free tier**: GPU type and availability vary; sometimes you get CPU until a GPU is free.
- **If `torch.cuda.is_available()` is False** after selecting GPU: wrong runtime, install is CPU-only torch, or session did not restart—fix runtime first, then reinstall torch with CUDA if needed (Colab’s default PyTorch build usually includes CUDA on GPU runtimes).

**Short answer:** enable GPU under **Runtime → Change runtime type**, confirm with **`!nvidia-smi`**, then set **`device="cuda"`** (or equivalent) in the models that support CUDA—TabPFN with `device="cuda"` is the direct “force GPU” knob for that part of your pipeline.

### What cannot use your NVIDIA CUDA stack

- **`sklearn`** pieces: **LogisticRegression**, **RandomForest**, **`GridSearchCV`**, **`CalibratedClassifierCV`**, metrics, threshold search, **§9 blends** (`P @ w`, stacking LR). These stay on **CPU**; that is normal and fine for this workload.
- **§9** only combines **already computed** probabilities; it does not add a second GPU path.

### What *can* use GPU (and is now wired in `advanced.ipynb`)

After **`import torch`** in **§1 setup**, the notebook sets:

- **`VLST_CUDA_AVAILABLE`** — `True` only if `torch.cuda.is_available()` **and** you did not disable boosters with env (see below).
- **CatBoost**: `task_type=CATBOOST_TASK_TYPE` → **`"GPU"`** or **`"CPU"`** on both the grid model and **`best_cat`**.
- **XGBoost**: `tree_method="hist"` and **`device=XGBOOST_DEVICE`** → **`"cuda"`** or **`"cpu"`** in the grid and **`best_xgb`**.
- **LightGBM**: **`device=LIGHTGBM_DEVICE`** → **`"gpu"`** when CUDA is on and you did not force CPU for LightGBM only (see below).

Setup prints one line, e.g.  
`Boosters: GPU | CatBoost task_type= GPU | XGBoost device= cuda | LightGBM device= gpu`.

### Env overrides (optional)

| Variable | Effect |
|----------|--------|
| **`VLST_USE_GPU_BOOSTERS=0`** (or `false` / `off` / `cpu`) | Force **CPU** for CatBoost / XGB / LGB regardless of CUDA. |
| **`VLST_LIGHTGBM_DEVICE=cpu`** | Keep Cat/XGB on GPU (if CUDA) but force **LightGBM on CPU** (handy if your `pip install lightgbm` is **CPU-only** and `device=gpu` errors). |
| **`VLST_LIGHTGBM_DEVICE=gpu`** | Force **GPU** for LightGBM even when you tune the main flag. |

### LightGBM caveat

Many **pip** installs of LightGBM are **CPU-only**. If you get an error about GPU / OpenCL / CUDA when **LightGBM** runs, set:

`VLST_LIGHTGBM_DEVICE=cpu`  
(or install a **CUDA-enabled** LightGBM build for your platform).

### TabPFN (unchanged by this patch)

TabPFN is still controlled in **§8b** (e.g. `TABPFN_DEVICE` / your earlier `torch`-based logic there). The new block in **§1** only names **`VLST_*`** for **CatBoost / XGBoost / LightGBM**.

**Summary:** the “rest” of the heavy training that *benefits* from CUDA is now the **three gradient boosting libraries**; everything **sklearn**-shaped and **§9** stays CPU by design.

### ASSISTANT
Here’s what is going on.

## The §9 “table” is not from `print`

In your notebook, the blend summary is shown with **`display(...)`** on a **DataFrame**, not with `print(...)`. In Jupyter / Colab that usually renders as an **HTML table** in the cell output.

The line you remember — the **“Tradeoff (test — for diagnostics only)…”** text — comes from a **`print(...)` farther down in the same big §9 code cell**, **after** the table is built and displayed.

So in a normal run the order is roughly:

1. Earlier prints / work  
2. **`blend_table = …`**  
3. **`display(blend_table[...])`** ← **table should appear here**  
4. **`blend_table.to_csv(...)`**  
5. More logic  
6. **`print("Tradeoff …")`** ← **this is what you noticed at the bottom**

If you only notice the tradeoff line, very often it’s because you’re looking at the **bottom** of a **long** cell output, or the environment **truncated** earlier output.

## Common cases

1. **Long output / truncation (especially Colab)**  
   Very long stdout can cause the UI to **drop or hide the top** of the output. The **HTML `display` block can be above the cutoff**, while the final **`print("Tradeoff…")`** is still visible at the end.

2. **Scroll / collapsed output**  
   In VS Code or Jupyter, earlier output (including the DataFrame) can be **above** the visible area or in a **collapsed** region.

3. **The table is still on disk**  
   Right after `display`, the notebook does  
   `blend_table.to_csv(..., "ensemble_blend_comparison.csv")`.  
   So even if the on-screen table is easy to miss, you should still have  
   `data/result/modeling_advanced/ensemble_blend_comparison.csv` (or your `RESULT_DIR`) — open that in the IDE or with `pd.read_csv(...)`.

## Quick checks you can run

After §9 finishes:

```python
import pandas as pd
from pathlib import Path
p = Path("../../data/result/modeling_advanced/ensemble_blend_comparison.csv")  # adjust if your cwd differs
print(p.resolve(), p.exists(), p.stat().st_size if p.exists() else 0)
print(pd.read_csv(p).head())
```

If the file exists and has rows, the table **did** run; it was mostly a **display / scrolling / truncation** issue.

## If you want the table always visible as plain text

Add right after the existing `display(...)`:

```python
print(blend_table[[c for c in _blend_display_cols if c in blend_table.columns]].round(4).to_string())
```

That duplicates the table as **text** so it survives truncation better than HTML-only output.

**Bottom line:** the “Tradeoff…” message does **not** replace the table; it’s printed **later**. You’re almost certainly missing the **earlier** `display` output or it was **truncated**; confirm with the CSV or an explicit `print(...to_string())`.

Here is **why `TABPFN_BLEND_AVAILABLE` stays `False`**, and what we changed in the notebook.

## Why it is `False`

In **section 8b** the flag is only set to **`True`** after **all** of this succeeds:

1. **`RUN_TABPFN_BLEND`** is `True` (you already have that).
2. **`os.environ["TABPFN_TOKEN"]`** is set and **non-empty**.  
   If it is missing, the notebook **skips** TabPFN and leaves **`TABPFN_BLEND_AVAILABLE = False`**.  
   On **Kaggle**, `getpass` in §1b often **does not** give you a reliable way to set env vars the way it does locally, and nothing sets `TABPFN_TOKEN` unless **you** do (Secrets, env in notebook settings, etc.).
3. **`TabPFNClassifier(...).fit(...)`** and the calibration / `predict_proba` steps **finish without error**.  
   If anything here raises, the flag never becomes `True` (previously you might only see a traceback and still wonder about the flag).

So in practice on Kaggle it is almost always **(2) no token in the environment**, sometimes **(3) fit/API error**.

## What we changed (cell **23**, section 8b)

- **Before** checking the token: if `TABPFN_TOKEN` is not set, try **Kaggle Secrets**  
  `UserSecretsClient().get_secret("TABPFN_TOKEN")`  
  so a secret with that **exact name** fills `os.environ["TABPFN_TOKEN"]`.
- Wrapped the TabPFN **import + fit + calibrate** block in **`try` / `except`**: on failure it prints  
  **`TabPFN failed; TABPFN_BLEND_AVAILABLE stays False. Error: ...`**  
  so you see a clear reason instead of only inferring from the flag.

**Kaggle setup:** Add-ons → **Secrets** → add secret name **`TABPFN_TOKEN`** (same spelling) with your PriorLabs token. Internet must be allowed if the API needs it.

### ASSISTANT

### ASSISTANT
### What the line was trying to say

**“F1-max threshold chosen on cal only”** means: the value **t = 0.350** was picked by scanning thresholds **only on the calibration set** so that **F1 on cal** is as large as possible. It is **not** chosen to maximize F1 on the test set.

So:

- **On cal:** t = 0.350 **does** beat t = 0.50 for F1 (0.8696 vs 0.8000). That is exactly what “F1-max on cal” means.
- **On test:** you still use **that same** t = 0.350. The test split is a **different** sample, so the threshold that is best for cal is **not** guaranteed to be best for test. In your log, **test F1 is higher at t = 0.50** (0.7429) than at t = 0.350 (0.6977). That is **normal** and **not** a bug.

So there is **no contradiction**: “F1-max on cal” only promises a good **cal** F1 at that t; it does **not** promise the best **test** F1 among all thresholds.

This matches how you want to **deploy**: you tune the threshold on held-out **cal** data, then you **freeze** t and evaluate on **test** without searching t on test (that would be optimistic / leaky for real use).

### How to read your numbers

| Line | Meaning |
|------|--------|
| `@cal t=0.350 (F1-max on cal)` | Best F1 on **cal** in the grid → here F1 = **0.8696**. |
| `@test t=0.350 (F1-max on cal)` | Same **t = 0.350** on **test** → F1 = **0.6977** (not “max test F1”). |
| `@test t=0.50` | A **fixed** comparison point; here it happens to give **higher test F1** than 0.350. |

### Notebook change

The footnote in **cell 23** was updated so it explicitly says that the **test** row uses the **cal-tuned** t without tuning on test, so **test F1 can be lower** than at another threshold (e.g. 0.50 in your run).

2. **Code (cell 27)** — Uses **`scipy.optimize.minimize`** with **SLSQP** to find weights **w ≥ 0**, **∑w = 1**, that **maximize PR-AUC on `y_cal`** for the blended scores **`P_cal @ w`** (same 3 columns as §9: Cat, XGB, RF). It prints:
   - optimizer success / iterations  
   - **weights `[cat, xgb, rf]`**  
   - **PR-AUC** on cal & test and **ROC-AUC** on test for that blend  

If **`P_cal_4` / `P_test_4`** exist (TabPFN was on in §9), it runs the same optimization for the **4-way** stack and prints **`[cat, xgb, rf, tabpfn]`**.

It reuses your **`blend`** helper from §9 so behavior matches the rest of the notebook.

### What it does *not* do

- It does **not** replace §9’s grids; it’s an extra diagnostic / alternative.
- Objective is **PR-AUC on cal only** (honest for picking weights); test metrics are **reporting only**.
- SLSQP can get stuck in a **local** optimum; starting point is **equal weights** (same as a simple baseline).

**`scipy`** is already in `requirements.txt` (`scipy>=1.10`).

### ASSISTANT
Here is what was going wrong, what we changed, and how it relates to “all models” and neural nets.

### Why you always saw **0.33 / 0.25** (it was not really “learning”)

The first version used **SLSQP** on **w** with **bounds + ∑w = 1**. For **average precision** (a ranking score), the objective is **awkward for smooth gradient-based solvers**: finite-difference gradients can be **~0** at a **symmetric** start **1/n**, so the solver can report **success in 1 iteration** and **never leave** the equal-weight point. So the weights were **not** a meaningful “learned” optimum; the optimizer effectively **stayed at initialization**.

### What we use now (same §9b cells **26–27**, content replaced)

1. **Softmax weights** `w = softmax(z)` with **unconstrained** `z` (always on the simplex, no equality constraint bug).
2. **L-BFGS-B** on **negative PR-AUC**, with **several random restarts** (not only `z = 0`).
3. **`differential_evolution`** over `z` in a box (then softmax) as a **global-ish** refinement / fallback.
4. **Wide matrix** of **sigmoid-calibrated** probabilities (same idea as §8): **Cat, XGB, RF, LGB, LR**, and **TabPFN** if `p_tabpfn_cal` / `p_tabpfn_test` exist — so you are not limited to the three columns of `P_cal`.
5. **Optional PyTorch**: **Adam** on `softmax(logits)` minimizing **BCE** on cal — this is a small “neural” **only in the sense of gradient descent on softmax logits**; it optimizes **BCE**, **not** PR-AUC (documented in the markdown). Use it as a **different** learned mixer, not a drop-in for PR-AUC.

Globals saved: **`LEARNED_WIDE_BLEND_WEIGHTS_PR_AUC`**, **`LEARNED_WIDE_BLEND_INFO`**.

There is also a short block that reruns the **same PR-AUC objective** on the **original 3-column `P_cal`** so you can compare to the wide stack.

### Why we didn’t “overlook” the other models before

Section **9** only ever built **`P_cal` = [Cat, XGB, RF]** (`ens_models`). **LGB** and **LR** are trained earlier but were **not** in that stack. The updated §9b **adds** them (and TabPFN when available) by **calibrating** `best_lgb` / `best_lr` on the cal slice like the other models.

### Neural network for weights — tradeoffs

- A **one-layer “network”** that outputs **fixed** blend weights is basically **softmax(logits)** — what we optimize in scipy **or** in the optional PyTorch BCE block. A deeper MLP **without per-sample features** does not add much over that for **global** weights.
- **§9 already has `LogisticRegression` stacking** on the base probabilities: that **learns** coefficients (can be **negative** or >1 in logit space — **not** a pure convex blend). That is often stronger than a **nonnegative** mix; it’s a different modeling choice.
- If you want a **real** neural **meta-learner**, you typically need **more data** or **nested CV**; otherwise it overfits the cal slice quickly.

Re-run **§9b** after **§8–9**; you should see **non-uniform** weights when PR-AUC can be improved, plus the **wide** and optional **BCE-torch** lines.

### ASSISTANT
Here’s what went wrong and what we changed.

### Cause

`sklearn.base.clone(best_lgb)` builds a **new, unfitted** `LGBMClassifier`. Your `calibrate_prefit` helper then does:

```python
if not hasattr(base_fitted, "classes_"):
    base_fitted.classes_ = np.unique(y_cal)
```

On an unfitted / cloned LightGBM model, `classes_` is handled in a way that **does not allow assignment**, so you get:

`AttributeError: property 'classes_' of 'LGBMClassifier' object has no setter`

Section **8** already uses **fitted** models: `calibrate_prefit(best_cat, ...)`, etc. LGB/LR should follow the same pattern: **`calibrate_prefit(best_lgb, ...)`** and **`calibrate_prefit(best_lr, ...)`**, not `clone(...)`.

### What’s in the repo now

1. **`build_wide_calibrated_probs`** (§9b) uses **`best_lgb`** and **`best_lr`** directly, with a short comment that **`clone()` is unfitted** and breaks LGBM.
2. **`calibrate_prefit`** (§8 cell) wraps `classes_` / `n_features_in_` assignment in **`try/except`** so odd edge cases don’t crash as easily (you still need a **fitted** base for calibration to be valid).
3. §9b **markdown** no longer says to use `clone` for LGB/LR; the extra **`from sklearn.base import clone`** import was removed from §9b.

### What you should do on Kaggle

Re-copy the updated notebook (or at least replace the §9b block and `calibrate_prefit` with the versions above). Any cell that still has:

`calibrate_prefit(clone(best_lgb), ...)`  

should be changed to:

`calibrate_prefit(best_lgb, ...)`  

(and the same for `best_lr`).

### ASSISTANT
Here is what changed in `advanced.ipynb` for **section 9c**.

### One section, one master table
Section **9c** is now a **single comparison table** that combines:

1. **`A_objectives` (optimization on cal, 4-way simplex)**  
   - **PR-AUC**: softmax + L-BFGS-B + `differential_evolution` (same family as 9b, seed 42 — may differ slightly from `learned_fourway_pr_auc_on_cal` in 9b).  
   - **MSE**: minimize mean \((\hat p - y)^2\) on cal.  
   - **BCE / log-loss**: minimize binary cross-entropy on cal.  
   - **F1 on cal**: Dirichlet random weights + threshold grid on cal (stochastic search).  
   - **Precision at t = 0.5** on cal: Dirichlet random weights.

2. **`C_meta_learners` (non–log-reg-only stackers on the four probs)**  
   - **SGDClassifier** `loss="log_loss"`, **elastic-net** penalty.  
   - **Ridge** on the four columns, then **sigmoid** to \([0,1]\).  
   - **HistGradientBoostingClassifier** (small depth/iterations), in **try/except** so a bad cal slice does not break the cell.  
   - Section **9** still supplies **logistic stacking** as `four_stacking_lr_on_probs_cal`, so LR is not duplicated here.

3. **`B_*` / `D_*` from `blend_table`**  
   - All **TabPFN-involved** rows from sections **9** and **9b** are merged in, tagged as:  
     - `B_grids_penalties_oof` — grids, tails, thirds, `tabpfn_only`, `four_*`, `three_way_*`, pair mixes, etc.  
     - `B_learned_from_9b` — variants starting with `learned_`.  
     - `D_blend_table` — anything else that passes the TabPFN mask.

New columns: **`comparison_block`** (which family of method) and **`notes`** (JSON for the two search-based A-rows).

### Outputs
- **`tabpfn_comprehensive_comparison.csv`** — full master table.  
- **`TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF`** — same data in memory.  
- **`tabpfn_mix_comparison.csv`** — short legacy subset (key variants only).

### Markdown
The **9c** markdown cell describes blocks **A / B / C / D** and the run order (**8b → 9 → optional 9b → 9c**). The **9b** footer line now points to this comprehensive table.

If you want **extra** blocks later (e.g. **recall@fixed-precision**, **explicit four-way diversity re-search**, or **wide-stack** TabPFN rows inside the same CSV), say which metrics matter most and we can add them without bloating runtime too much.

```markdown
## 9c. TabPFN — comprehensive comparison (single master table)

This section mirrors the **ensemble-style tools** from section 9 on the **four calibrated probabilities** **[Cat, XGB, RF, TabPFN]** (`P_cal_4` / `P_test_4` from section 9 when TabPFN is enabled).

**What the code adds (column `comparison_block`):**

- **`A_objectives`** — Optimizers on the **probability simplex**: **PR-AUC** (softmax + L-BFGS-B + differential evolution, same spirit as 9b), **MSE** to cal labels, **binary log-loss (BCE)**, **Dirichlet random search** for high **F1 on cal** (with a threshold grid), and for high **precision at t = 0.5** on cal.
- **`C_meta_learners`** — Models fit on the four prob columns only: **SGD log-loss with elastic-net**, **Ridge** scores passed through a **sigmoid**, and **HistGradientBoosting** (wrapped in try/except). Section 9 already contributes **logistic stacking** as `four_stacking_lr_on_probs_cal` in the merged `blend_table`.
- **`B_grids_penalties_oof`**, **`B_learned_from_9b`**, **`D_blend_table`** — Rows **re-used from `blend_table`** (after sections 9 and optional 9b): simplex grids, min-weight / diversity / OOF PR winners, learned blends, and hand TabPFN mixes.

**Run order:** 8b → 9 → optional 9b → **this cell**. Saves **`tabpfn_comprehensive_comparison.csv`** and exposes **`TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF`**. A smaller **`tabpfn_mix_comparison.csv`** is still written for a short curated subset.
```

### Cell B — **Code** (replace the entire old §9c code cell)

```python
# --- Section 9c: TabPFN-inclusive comprehensive comparison (single master table) ---
# Run after sections 9 and 8b (TabPFN). Section 9b optional (adds learned rows to blend_table).
#
# Structure (see markdown above this cell):
#   A — Convex / smooth objectives on the 4-way simplex [Cat, XGB, RF, TabPFN] (cal-only fit)
#   B — Same family as section 9: reuse grid / penalty / OOF winners (already in blend_table)
#   C — Meta-learners on the four probabilities (not only logistic regression)
#   D — Hand diagnostic mixes from blend_table
#   E — Master table = A + C + curated rows from blend_table (TabPFN-involved), deduped by variant

import os
import json

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy.optimize import differential_evolution, minimize
from scipy.special import expit

from sklearn.metrics import (
    accuracy_score,
    average_precision_score,
    f1_score,
    precision_score,
    recall_score,
    roc_auc_score,
)
from sklearn.linear_model import Ridge, SGDClassifier
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier

def _sm(z):
    z = np.asarray(z, dtype=float).ravel()
    z = z - np.max(z)
    e = np.exp(z)
    return e / (np.sum(e) + 1e-15)

def _learn_pr_simplex(P, y, n_restarts=8, seed=42):
    """Maximize PR-AUC on cal; softmax(z) on 4-simplex."""

    def neg(z):
        w = _sm(z)
        p = np.clip((P @ w).ravel(), 1e-12, 1.0 - 1e-12)
        return -float(average_precision_score(np.asarray(y).astype(int).ravel(), p))

    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)
    best_z, best_v = None, np.inf
    for z0 in [np.zeros(4)] + [rng.standard_normal(4) for _ in range(n_restarts - 1)]:
        r = minimize(neg, z0, method="L-BFGS-B", options={"maxiter": 400, "ftol": 1e-10})
        if r.fun < best_v:
            best_v, best_z = r.fun, r.x

    def _obj(zf):
        return neg(np.asarray(zf, dtype=float))

    de = differential_evolution(_obj, [(-4.0, 4.0)] * 4, seed=seed, maxiter=100, polish=True)
    if de.fun < best_v:
        best_v, best_z = de.fun, de.x
    return _sm(best_z)

def _learn_smooth(P, y, loss_fn, n_restarts=6, seed=43):
    """Minimize loss_fn(P @ sm(z), y) over z."""

    def obj(z):
        w = _sm(z)
        p = np.clip((P @ w).ravel(), 1e-12, 1.0 - 1e-12)
        return float(loss_fn(p, y))

    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)
    best_z, best_v = None, np.inf
    for z0 in [np.zeros(4)] + [rng.standard_normal(4) for _ in range(n_restarts - 1)]:
        r = minimize(obj, z0, method="L-BFGS-B", options={"maxiter": 400, "ftol": 1e-11})
        if r.fun < best_v:
            best_v, best_z = r.fun, r.x

    de = differential_evolution(
        lambda z: obj(np.asarray(z, dtype=float)),
        [(-4.0, 4.0)] * 4,
        seed=seed + 1,
        maxiter=90,
        polish=True,
    )
    if de.fun < best_v:
        best_v, best_z = de.fun, de.x
    return _sm(best_z)

def _row_blend_schema(
    variant,
    block,
    p_c,
    p_te,
    w4,
    y_cal_arr,
    y_te_arr,
    t_grid,
    w_lgb=np.nan,
    w_lr=np.nan,
    notes="",
):
    """Match section 9 blend_table columns (+ comparison_block, notes)."""
    t_opt = best_threshold_f1_on_cal(y_cal_arr, p_c, t_grid)
    y_hat = (p_te >= t_opt).astype(int)
    y_te_arr = np.asarray(y_te_arr).astype(int).ravel()
    w4 = np.asarray(w4, dtype=float).ravel()
    return {
        "comparison_block": block,
        "variant": variant,
        "w_cat": float(w4[0]) if np.isfinite(w4[0]) else np.nan,
        "w_xgb": float(w4[1]) if np.isfinite(w4[1]) else np.nan,
        "w_rf": float(w4[2]) if np.isfinite(w4[2]) else np.nan,
        "w_tabpfn": float(w4[3]) if np.isfinite(w4[3]) else np.nan,
        "w_lgb": float(w_lgb),
        "w_lr": float(w_lr),
        "pr_auc_cal": float(average_precision_score(y_cal_arr, p_c)),
        "max_f2_cal": float(max_f2_over_thresholds(y_cal_arr, p_c, t_grid)),
        "pr_auc_test": float(average_precision_score(y_te_arr, p_te)),
        "roc_auc_test": float(roc_auc_score(y_te_arr, p_te)),
        "precision": float(precision_score(y_te_arr, y_hat, zero_division=0)),
        "recall": float(recall_score(y_te_arr, y_hat, zero_division=0)),
        "f1_score": float(f1_score(y_te_arr, y_hat, zero_division=0)),
        "accuracy": float(accuracy_score(y_te_arr, y_hat)),
        "threshold_f1max_cal": float(t_opt),
        "notes": notes or "",
    }

def _random_search_max_f1(P, y, t_grid, n_dir=400, seed=7):
    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)
    best_f1, best_w, best_t = -1.0, np.ones(4) / 4.0, 0.5
    y = np.asarray(y).astype(int).ravel()
    for _ in range(n_dir):
        w = rng.dirichlet(np.ones(4))
        p = blend(P, w)
        for t in t_grid:
            fv = f1_score(y, (p >= t).astype(int), zero_division=0)
            if fv > best_f1:
                best_f1, best_w, best_t = fv, w, float(t)
    return best_w, best_t, float(best_f1)

def _random_search_max_precision_t05(P, y, n_dir=500, seed=11):
    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)
    best_pr, best_w = -1.0, np.ones(4) / 4.0
    y = np.asarray(y).astype(int).ravel()
    for _ in range(n_dir):
        w = rng.dirichlet(np.ones(4))
        p = blend(P, w)
        y_hat = (p >= 0.5).astype(int)
        pr = precision_score(y, y_hat, zero_division=0)
        if pr > best_pr:
            best_pr, best_w = pr, w
    return best_w, float(best_pr)

def _stack_probs_row(name, block, clf, Pc, Pt, y_cal_arr, y_te_arr, t_grid):
    clf.fit(Pc, y_cal_arr)
    if hasattr(clf, "predict_proba"):
        p_c = clf.predict_proba(Pc)[:, 1]
        p_t = clf.predict_proba(Pt)[:, 1]
    else:
        zc = clf.predict(Pc)
        zt = clf.predict(Pt)
        p_c = expit(zc)
        p_t = expit(zt)
    return _row_blend_schema(
        name,
        block,
        p_c,
        p_t,
        np.full(4, np.nan),
        y_cal_arr,
        y_te_arr,
        t_grid,
    )

TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF = None

if "blend_table" not in globals():
    print("Run section 9 first (blend_table).")
elif not globals().get("TABPFN_BLEND_AVAILABLE", False):
    print("TabPFN four-way blends are off (run section 8b; TABPFN_BLEND_AVAILABLE).")
elif globals().get("P_cal_4") is None or globals().get("P_test_4") is None:
    print("P_cal_4 / P_test_4 missing; run section 9 with TabPFN enabled.")
else:
    Pc = np.asarray(P_cal_4, dtype=float)
    Pt = np.asarray(P_test_4, dtype=float)
    yc = np.asarray(y_cal).astype(int).ravel()
    ye = np.asarray(y_test).astype(int).ravel()
    tg = t_grid_blend

    built = []

    # ----- A: objectives on 4-simplex -----
    w_pr = _learn_pr_simplex(Pc, yc, n_restarts=8, seed=42)
    built.append(
        _row_blend_schema(
            "A_opt_fourway_pr_auc_softmax_cal",
            "A_objectives",
            blend(Pc, w_pr),
            blend(Pt, w_pr),
            w_pr,
            yc,
            ye,
            tg,
        )
    )

    w_mse = _learn_smooth(
        Pc,
        yc,
        lambda p, y: np.mean((np.asarray(p).ravel() - np.asarray(y, dtype=float).ravel()) ** 2),
        seed=44,
    )
    built.append(
        _row_blend_schema(
            "A_opt_fourway_mse_softmax_cal",
            "A_objectives",
            blend(Pc, w_mse),
            blend(Pt, w_mse),
            w_mse,
            yc,
            ye,
            tg,
        )
    )

    def _bce(p, y):
        p = np.clip(np.asarray(p, dtype=float).ravel(), 1e-12, 1.0 - 1e-12)
        y = np.asarray(y, dtype=float).ravel()
        return -float(np.mean(y * np.log(p) + (1.0 - y) * np.log(1.0 - p)))

    w_bce = _learn_smooth(Pc, yc, _bce, seed=45)
    built.append(
        _row_blend_schema(
            "A_opt_fourway_neg_logloss_bce_softmax_cal",
            "A_objectives",
            blend(Pc, w_bce),
            blend(Pt, w_bce),
            w_bce,
            yc,
            ye,
            tg,
        )
    )

    w_f1, t_f1, f1v = _random_search_max_f1(Pc, yc, tg, n_dir=350, seed=46)
    built.append(
        _row_blend_schema(
            "A_search_fourway_max_f1_cal_dirichlet",
            "A_objectives",
            blend(Pc, w_f1),
            blend(Pt, w_f1),
            w_f1,
            yc,
            ye,
            tg,
            notes=json.dumps(
                {"cal_f1_at_best_t": round(f1v, 4), "threshold_used_for_f1_search": t_f1}
            ),
        )
    )

    w_prec, prec_v = _random_search_max_precision_t05(Pc, yc, n_dir=450, seed=47)
    built.append(
        _row_blend_schema(
            "A_search_fourway_max_precision_t0p5_cal_dirichlet",
            "A_objectives",
            blend(Pc, w_prec),
            blend(Pt, w_prec),
            w_prec,
            yc,
            ye,
            tg,
            notes=json.dumps({"cal_precision_at_0.5": round(prec_v, 4)}),
        )
    )

    # ----- C: meta-learners on P4 (weights N/A; leave w_* as NaN) -----
    built.append(
        _stack_probs_row(
            "C_meta_sgd_logloss_elasticnet_on_P4_cal",
            "C_meta_learners",
            SGDClassifier(
                loss="log_loss",
                penalty="elasticnet",
                l1_ratio=0.2,
                alpha=0.02,
                max_iter=2500,
                random_state=42,
                class_weight="balanced",
            ),
            Pc,
            Pt,
            yc,
            ye,
            tg,
        )
    )
    built.append(
        _stack_probs_row(
            "C_meta_ridge_sigmoid_on_P4_cal",
            "C_meta_learners",
            Ridge(alpha=2.0, random_state=42),
            Pc,
            Pt,
            yc,
            ye,
            tg,
        )
    )
    try:
        built.append(
            _stack_probs_row(
                "C_meta_histgb_on_P4_cal",
                "C_meta_learners",
                HistGradientBoostingClassifier(
                    max_depth=2,
                    max_iter=120,
                    learning_rate=0.08,
                    random_state=42,
                    class_weight="balanced",
                ),
                Pc,
                Pt,
                yc,
                ye,
                tg,
            )
        )
    except Exception as _eh:
        print("HistGB meta-learner skipped:", repr(_eh))

    extra_df = pd.DataFrame(built)
    for c in extra_df.columns:
        if c == "notes":
            continue
        if extra_df[c].dtype == object:
            continue
        extra_df[c] = pd.to_numeric(extra_df[c], errors="coerce")

    # ----- E: merge with TabPFN-involved blend_table rows -----
    bt = blend_table.copy()

    _B_PREFIXES = (
        "four_equal",
        "four_grid",
        "four_nocat",
        "four_stacking",
        "tabpfn_only",
        "three_way_",
        "cat_tabpfn",
        "xgb_tabpfn",
        "rf_tabpfn",
        "xgb_rf_tabpfn",
        "cat_xgb_tabpfn",
        "cat_rf_tabpfn",
    )

    def _infer_block(v):
        v = str(v)
        if v.startswith("learned_"):
            return "B_learned_from_9b"
        if v.startswith(_B_PREFIXES):
            return "B_grids_penalties_oof"
        return "D_blend_table"

    bt["comparison_block"] = bt["variant"].map(_infer_block)
    if "notes" not in bt.columns:
        bt["notes"] = ""

    mask_tp = (
        (bt["w_tabpfn"].notna() & (bt["w_tabpfn"] > 1e-8))
        | (bt["variant"].astype(str) == "tabpfn_only_calibrated")
        | (bt["variant"].astype(str).str.startswith("learned_wide"))
        | (bt["variant"].astype(str).str.startswith("learned_fourway"))
    )
    bt_sub = bt.loc[mask_tp].copy()

    keys = set(extra_df["variant"].astype(str))
    bt_sub = bt_sub[~bt_sub["variant"].astype(str).isin(keys)]

    cols_order = [
        "comparison_block",
        "variant",
        "w_cat",
        "w_xgb",
        "w_rf",
        "w_tabpfn",
        "w_lgb",
        "w_lr",
        "pr_auc_cal",
        "max_f2_cal",
        "pr_auc_test",
        "roc_auc_test",
        "precision",
        "recall",
        "f1_score",
        "accuracy",
        "threshold_f1max_cal",
        "notes",
    ]
    for c in cols_order:
        if c not in bt_sub.columns and c != "notes":
            bt_sub[c] = np.nan
    if "notes" not in bt_sub.columns:
        bt_sub["notes"] = ""

    master = pd.concat([extra_df, bt_sub], ignore_index=True, sort=False)

    block_rank = {
        "A_objectives": 0,
        "C_meta_learners": 1,
        "B_learned_from_9b": 2,
        "B_grids_penalties_oof": 3,
        "D_blend_table": 4,
    }
    master["_blk"] = master["comparison_block"].map(lambda x: block_rank.get(str(x), 9))
    master = master.sort_values(["_blk", "pr_auc_test"], ascending=[True, False]).drop(columns=["_blk"])

    TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF = master[[c for c in cols_order if c in master.columns]]

    _view = TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF.copy()
    for _c in _view.columns:
        if _view[_c].dtype == object:
            continue
        _view[_c] = _view[_c].astype(float).round(4)

    try:
        from IPython.display import HTML, display as _d9c

        _h = _view.to_html(index=False, escape=True)
        _d9c(
            HTML(
                "<style>"
                ".vlst-tp{font-size:15px;line-height:1.35}"
                ".vlst-tp-x{width:100%;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:visible;margin:10px 0;padding:8px;"
                "border:1px solid #ccc;background:#fff;box-sizing:border-box}"
                ".vlst-tp table{border-collapse:collapse;font-size:15px;white-space:nowrap}"
                ".vlst-tp th,.vlst-tp td{border:1px solid #bbb;padding:8px 12px;vertical-align:top}"
                ".vlst-tp thead th{position:sticky;top:0;background:#e8f5e9;z-index:3;text-align:left}"
                ".vlst-tp td:first-child,.vlst-tp th:first-child{font-weight:700;background:#f1f8e9;"
                "position:sticky;left:0;z-index:2;box-shadow:2px 0 4px rgba(0,0,0,.05)}"
                "</style>"
                "<h4 style=\"margin:8px 0;font-size:16px\">Section 9c — TabPFN comprehensive master table</h4>"
                "<p style=\"font-size:15px;margin:6px 0\"><b>comparison_block:</b> "
                "<code>A_objectives</code> = softmax / search optimizers on cal; "
                "<code>C_meta_learners</code> = classifiers on the four calibrated probs; "
                "<code>B_grids_penalties_oof</code> / <code>B_learned_from_9b</code> / <code>D_blend_table</code> "
                "= rows from section 9/9b that involve TabPFN. Convex rows show weights on [Cat, XGB, RF, TabPFN]; "
                "meta-learners leave weights blank.</p>"
                "<div class=\"vlst-tp vlst-tp-x\">" + _h + "</div>"
            )
        )
    except Exception as _e:
        print("HTML display skipped:", repr(_e))

    print("\n=== Section 9c — plain text ===\n")
    print(_view.to_string(index=False))

    _out = os.path.join(RESULT_DIR, "tabpfn_comprehensive_comparison.csv")
    TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF.to_csv(_out, index=False)
    print("\nSaved:", _out)

    try:
        vo = [
            "tabpfn_only_calibrated",
            "four_equal_1/4",
            "learned_fourway_pr_auc_on_cal",
            "four_grid_max_pr_auc_cal",
            "four_stacking_lr_on_probs_cal",
        ]
        k = "four_grid_mean_{}fold_oof_pr_cal".format(int(globals().get("BLEND_CV_FOLDS", 5)))
        if k not in vo:
            vo.insert(3, k)
        present = [v for v in vo if v in set(TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF["variant"].astype(str))]
        _legacy = os.path.join(RESULT_DIR, "tabpfn_mix_comparison.csv")
        TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF[TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF["variant"].isin(present)].to_csv(
            _legacy, index=False
        )
        print("Also wrote legacy subset:", _legacy)
    except Exception as _e2:
        print("(Legacy tabpfn_mix_comparison export skipped:", repr(_e2), ")")
```

### ASSISTANT
Below is a **single code cell** you can paste **after** section 9 (and 8b TabPFN). It does **not** use F2. It assigns each candidate to a **subtype block**, picks the variant with **highest precision on cal** (threshold searched on `t_grid_blend`), then builds **one equal mix** of those winners’ probabilities and picks a **final threshold on cal** again by **max precision**.

The file also lives at `code/modeling/snippets/section_9c_precision_mix_cell.py`.

---

### Why not RF / XGB as the default *meta* blender?

- **Very few meta-features** (here only four probabilities). Tree models need data to estimate splits; on a **small cal slice** they **overfit** easily and behave erratically.
- **Logistic regression** on calibrated probs is the usual stacking choice: **fast**, **stable**, **easy to regularize**, and weights are **easy to read**. It is not “the only” option: your §9c already uses **SGD (elastic-net), Ridge + sigmoid, HistGB** on the same four columns.
- **RF/XGB on probs** are possible if you **regularize hard** (shallow trees, strong `min_samples_leaf`, etc.) and accept more tuning; they do not automatically beat a linear meta layer on 4D.

If you want **XGBoost meta** explicitly, the pattern is: `XGBClassifier(n_estimators=30, max_depth=2, learning_rate=0.05, subsample=0.9, colsample_bytree=0.9, reg_lambda=5, reg_alpha=1, random_state=42)` fit on `P_cal_4`, then `predict_proba` — add it to `catalog` the same way as HistGB.

---

### Ready-to-paste cell (precision-only selection + equal mix)

```python
# --- After §9c (or standalone): per-block precision winners + one equal mix ---
# Requires: §9 (blend, P_cal_4, P_test_4, y_cal, y_test, t_grid_blend, blend_table, TABPFN_BLEND_AVAILABLE)
# Optional: TABPFN_COMPREHENSIVE_DF (if missing, uses TabPFN rows from blend_table + recomputes A/C probs)
#
# Selection: within each comparison_block, pick the variant with highest PRECISION on cal,
#            searching thresholds on t_grid_blend (no F2).
# Mix: equal average of winners' test (and cal) probabilities; final threshold on cal again maximizes precision.

import json
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy.optimize import differential_evolution, minimize
from scipy.special import expit
from sklearn.metrics import (
    accuracy_score,
    average_precision_score,
    f1_score,
    precision_score,
    recall_score,
    roc_auc_score,
)
from sklearn.linear_model import Ridge, SGDClassifier
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier

def _sm(z):
    z = np.asarray(z, dtype=float).ravel()
    z = z - np.max(z)
    e = np.exp(z)
    return e / (np.sum(e) + 1e-15)

def _best_precision_threshold(y_true, p, t_grid):
    """Return (t_best, precision_cal) maximizing precision on y_true (cal)."""
    y_true = np.asarray(y_true).astype(int).ravel()
    p = np.asarray(p, dtype=float).ravel()
    best_t, best_pr = 0.5, -1.0
    for t in t_grid:
        y_hat = (p >= float(t)).astype(int)
        pr = precision_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0)
        if pr > best_pr:
            best_pr, best_t = pr, float(t)
    return best_t, float(best_pr)

def _metrics_at_t(y_true, p, t):
    y_true = np.asarray(y_true).astype(int).ravel()
    p = np.asarray(p, dtype=float).ravel()
    y_hat = (p >= float(t)).astype(int)
    return {
        "precision": float(precision_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0)),
        "recall": float(recall_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0)),
        "f1": float(f1_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0)),
        "accuracy": float(accuracy_score(y_true, y_hat)),
    }

def _learn_pr_simplex(P, y, n_restarts=8, seed=42):
    def neg(z):
        w = _sm(z)
        pv = np.clip((P @ w).ravel(), 1e-12, 1.0 - 1e-12)
        return -float(average_precision_score(np.asarray(y).astype(int).ravel(), pv))

    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)
    best_z, best_v = None, np.inf
    for z0 in [np.zeros(4)] + [rng.standard_normal(4) for _ in range(n_restarts - 1)]:
        r = minimize(neg, z0, method="L-BFGS-B", options={"maxiter": 400, "ftol": 1e-10})
        if r.fun < best_v:
            best_v, best_z = r.fun, r.x

    def _obj(zf):
        return neg(np.asarray(zf, dtype=float))

    de = differential_evolution(_obj, [(-4.0, 4.0)] * 4, seed=seed, maxiter=100, polish=True)
    if de.fun < best_v:
        best_v, best_z = de.fun, de.x
    return _sm(best_z)

def _learn_smooth(P, y, loss_fn, n_restarts=6, seed=43):
    def obj(z):
        w = _sm(z)
        p = np.clip((P @ w).ravel(), 1e-12, 1.0 - 1e-12)
        return float(loss_fn(p, y))

    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)
    best_z, best_v = None, np.inf
    for z0 in [np.zeros(4)] + [rng.standard_normal(4) for _ in range(n_restarts - 1)]:
        r = minimize(obj, z0, method="L-BFGS-B", options={"maxiter": 400, "ftol": 1e-11})
        if r.fun < best_v:
            best_v, best_z = r.fun, r.x
    de = differential_evolution(
        lambda z: obj(np.asarray(z, dtype=float)),
        [(-4.0, 4.0)] * 4,
        seed=seed + 1,
        maxiter=90,
        polish=True,
    )
    if de.fun < best_v:
        best_v, best_z = de.fun, de.x
    return _sm(best_z)

def _bce_loss(p, y):
    p = np.clip(np.asarray(p, dtype=float).ravel(), 1e-12, 1.0 - 1e-12)
    y = np.asarray(y, dtype=float).ravel()
    return -float(np.mean(y * np.log(p) + (1.0 - y) * np.log(1.0 - p)))

def _stack_p4(clf, Pc, Pt, y_cal_arr):
    clf.fit(Pc, y_cal_arr)
    if hasattr(clf, "predict_proba"):
        return clf.predict_proba(Pc)[:, 1], clf.predict_proba(Pt)[:, 1]
    zc, zt = clf.predict(Pc), clf.predict(Pt)
    return expit(zc), expit(zt)

TABPFN_PRECISION_BLOCK_WINNERS = None
TABPFN_PRECISION_EQUAL_MIX = None

if not globals().get("TABPFN_BLEND_AVAILABLE"):
    print("TabPFN off — skip precision-mix cell.")
elif globals().get("P_cal_4") is None:
    print("Run section 9 first (P_cal_4).")
else:
    Pc = np.asarray(P_cal_4, dtype=float)
    Pt = np.asarray(P_test_4, dtype=float)
    yc = np.asarray(y_cal).astype(int).ravel()
    ye = np.asarray(y_test).astype(int).ravel()
    tg = t_grid_blend

    catalog = {}

    # --- Rebuild fixed A / C variants (same spirit as §9c; selection here is precision-based) ---
    w_pr = _learn_pr_simplex(Pc, yc, seed=42)
    catalog["A_opt_fourway_pr_auc_softmax_cal"] = (blend(Pc, w_pr), blend(Pt, w_pr))
    w_mse = _learn_smooth(
        Pc,
        yc,
        lambda p, y: np.mean((np.asarray(p).ravel() - np.asarray(y, dtype=float).ravel()) ** 2),
        seed=44,
    )
    catalog["A_opt_fourway_mse_softmax_cal"] = (blend(Pc, w_mse), blend(Pt, w_mse))
    w_bce = _learn_smooth(Pc, yc, _bce_loss, seed=45)
    catalog["A_opt_fourway_neg_logloss_bce_softmax_cal"] = (blend(Pc, w_bce), blend(Pt, w_bce))

    rngp = np.random.RandomState(47)
    best_pr, best_w = -1.0, np.ones(4) / 4.0
    for _ in range(450):
        w = rngp.dirichlet(np.ones(4))
        p = blend(Pc, w)
        y_hat = (p >= 0.5).astype(int)
        pr = precision_score(yc, y_hat, zero_division=0)
        if pr > best_pr:
            best_pr, best_w = pr, w
    catalog["A_search_fourway_max_precision_t0p5_cal_dirichlet"] = (
        blend(Pc, best_w),
        blend(Pt, best_w),
    )

    catalog["C_meta_sgd_logloss_elasticnet_on_P4_cal"] = _stack_p4(
        SGDClassifier(
            loss="log_loss",
            penalty="elasticnet",
            l1_ratio=0.2,
            alpha=0.02,
            max_iter=2500,
            random_state=42,
            class_weight="balanced",
        ),
        Pc,
        Pt,
        yc,
    )
    catalog["C_meta_ridge_sigmoid_on_P4_cal"] = _stack_p4(Ridge(alpha=2.0, random_state=42), Pc, Pt, yc)
    try:
        catalog["C_meta_histgb_on_P4_cal"] = _stack_p4(
            HistGradientBoostingClassifier(
                max_depth=2,
                max_iter=120,
                learning_rate=0.08,
                random_state=42,
                class_weight="balanced",
            ),
            Pc,
            Pt,
            yc,
        )
    except Exception as _e:
        print("HistGB skipped in catalog:", repr(_e))

    # --- All TabPFN-involved rows from blend_table (weights → convex blend) ---
    bt = blend_table.copy()
    mask_tp = (
        (bt["w_tabpfn"].notna() & (bt["w_tabpfn"] > 1e-8))
        | (bt["variant"].astype(str) == "tabpfn_only_calibrated")
        | (bt["variant"].astype(str).str.startswith("learned_wide"))
        | (bt["variant"].astype(str).str.startswith("learned_fourway"))
    )
    for _, row in bt.loc[mask_tp].iterrows():
        vn = str(row["variant"])
        if vn in catalog:
            continue
        wc = row.get("w_cat", np.nan)
        wx = row.get("w_xgb", np.nan)
        wrf = row.get("w_rf", np.nan)
        wt = row.get("w_tabpfn", np.nan)
        if not all(np.isfinite([wc, wx, wrf, wt])):
            continue
        w4 = np.array([float(wc), float(wx), float(wrf), float(wt)], dtype=float)
        if w4.sum() <= 0:
            continue
        catalog[vn] = (blend(Pc, w4), blend(Pt, w4))

    _B_PREFIXES = (
        "four_equal",
        "four_grid",
        "four_nocat",
        "four_stacking",
        "tabpfn_only",
        "three_way_",
        "cat_tabpfn",
        "xgb_tabpfn",
        "rf_tabpfn",
        "xgb_rf_tabpfn",
        "cat_xgb_tabpfn",
        "cat_rf_tabpfn",
    )

    def _infer_block(v):
        v = str(v)
        if v.startswith("learned_"):
            return "B_learned_from_9b"
        if v.startswith("A_opt") or v.startswith("A_search"):
            return "A_objectives"
        if v.startswith("C_meta"):
            return "C_meta_learners"
        if v.startswith(_B_PREFIXES):
            return "B_grids_penalties_oof"
        return "D_blend_table"

    # --- Per block: max precision on cal (best threshold on cal) ---
    rows = []
    by_block = {}
    for vn, (pc, pte) in catalog.items():
        blk = _infer_block(vn)
        t_star, pr_cal = _best_precision_threshold(yc, pc, tg)
        by_block.setdefault(blk, []).append((vn, pr_cal, t_star, pc, pte))

    winners = {}
    for blk, lst in by_block.items():
        lst.sort(key=lambda x: -x[1])
        vn, pr_cal, t_star, pc, pte = lst[0]
        winners[blk] = {
            "variant": vn,
            "block": blk,
            "precision_cal_at_best_t": round(pr_cal, 4),
            "threshold_max_precision_cal": round(t_star, 4),
            "pr_auc_cal": round(float(average_precision_score(yc, pc)), 4),
            "pr_auc_test": round(float(average_precision_score(ye, pte)), 4),
        }
        m_te = _metrics_at_t(ye, pte, t_star)
        rows.append(
            {
                "block": blk,
                "winner_variant": vn,
                "precision_cal": round(pr_cal, 4),
                "threshold_cal": round(t_star, 4),
                "precision_test@that_t": round(m_te["precision"], 4),
                "recall_test@that_t": round(m_te["recall"], 4),
                "f1_test@that_t": round(m_te["f1"], 4),
            }
        )

    win_df = pd.DataFrame(rows).sort_values("block")
    print("\n=== Per-block winner (max precision on cal; threshold grid on cal) ===\n")
    print(win_df.to_string(index=False))

    # --- Equal mix of winner probabilities ---
    p_cal_list = [catalog[winners[b]["variant"]][0] for b in sorted(winners.keys())]
    p_te_list = [catalog[winners[b]["variant"]][1] for b in sorted(winners.keys())]
    p_cal_mix = np.mean(np.stack(p_cal_list, axis=0), axis=0)
    p_te_mix = np.mean(np.stack(p_te_list, axis=0), axis=0)

    t_mix, pr_mix_cal = _best_precision_threshold(yc, p_cal_mix, tg)
    m_cal_mix = _metrics_at_t(yc, p_cal_mix, t_mix)
    m_te_mix = _metrics_at_t(ye, p_te_mix, t_mix)

    TABPFN_PRECISION_BLOCK_WINNERS = winners
    TABPFN_PRECISION_EQUAL_MIX = {
        "blocks_mixed": sorted(winners.keys()),
        "variants_mixed": [winners[b]["variant"] for b in sorted(winners.keys())],
        "threshold_max_precision_cal": t_mix,
        "precision_cal": pr_mix_cal,
        "metrics_cal@t": m_cal_mix,
        "metrics_test@t": m_te_mix,
        "roc_auc_test": float(roc_auc_score(ye, p_te_mix)),
        "pr_auc_test": float(average_precision_score(ye, p_te_mix)),
    }

    print("\n=== Equal mix of per-block precision winners ===")
    print("Blocks:", TABPFN_PRECISION_EQUAL_MIX["blocks_mixed"])
    print("Variants:", TABPFN_PRECISION_EQUAL_MIX["variants_mixed"])
    print("Threshold (max precision on cal):", round(t_mix, 4))
    print("Cal @t:", json.dumps({k: round(v, 4) for k, v in m_cal_mix.items()}))
    print("Test @t:", json.dumps({k: round(v, 4) for k, v in m_te_mix.items()}))
    print("Test PR-AUC:", round(TABPFN_PRECISION_EQUAL_MIX["pr_auc_test"], 4), "| ROC-AUC:", round(TABPFN_PRECISION_EQUAL_MIX["roc_auc_test"], 4))
```

**Globals written:** `TABPFN_PRECISION_BLOCK_WINNERS`, `TABPFN_PRECISION_EQUAL_MIX`.

**Caveat:** Maximizing **precision only** on a small cal set often drives **recall to zero** (predict almost no positives). If you need a floor on recall, say so and the objective can become “max precision subject to recall ≥ r” on the same threshold grid.

```python
from sklearn.model_selection import (
    GridSearchCV,
    RandomizedSearchCV,
    StratifiedKFold,
    train_test_split,
)
from sklearn.neural_network import MLPClassifier
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from scipy.stats import loguniform
```
