### 2) **New markdown cell** (after RF refit on `X_merge`, before §8)

```markdown
## 7b. Neural network (optional): randomized hyperparameter search + refit

Trains **`MLPClassifier`** in **`Pipeline(StandardScaler, MLP)`** on **`X_fit`** with **`RandomizedSearchCV`** (stratified CV, **`average_precision`**), then **refits on `X_merge`**. **§8** sigmoid-calibrates on **`X_cal`** → **`cal_nn`**.

**Toggles:** `RUN_NN_BLEND` (default **False**), `NN_RANDOM_TRIALS`, `NN_SEARCH_CV`, `NN_MAX_ITER`, or env **`VLST_RUN_NN_BLEND=1`**.

**Note:** sklearn MLP has no **dropout**; use **`alpha`** (L2) and **early stopping**.

With **`NN_BLEND_AVAILABLE`**, **§9** adds **[Cat, XGB, RF, NN]** four-way blends; with **TabPFN** too, **five-way** rows (Dirichlet search, stacking, hand mixes).
```

---

### 3) **New code cell** (right after that markdown)

```python
# --- §7b MLP: RandomizedSearchCV on X_fit, refit on X_merge (calibration in §8) ---
RUN_NN_BLEND = False  # or VLST_RUN_NN_BLEND=1
_nn_env = (os.environ.get("VLST_RUN_NN_BLEND", "") or "").strip().lower()
if _nn_env in ("1", "true", "yes", "on"):
    RUN_NN_BLEND = True

NN_RANDOM_TRIALS = int(os.environ.get("VLST_NN_RANDOM_TRIALS", "12"))
NN_SEARCH_CV = max(2, int(os.environ.get("VLST_NN_SEARCH_CV", "2")))
NN_MAX_ITER = int(os.environ.get("VLST_NN_MAX_ITER", "400"))

NN_BLEND_AVAILABLE = False
best_nn_merge = None
nn_search = None

if not RUN_NN_BLEND:
    print(
        "§7b NN: OFF (set RUN_NN_BLEND = True or VLST_RUN_NN_BLEND=1 for MLP search + refit on X_merge)."
    )
else:
    _nn_base = MLPClassifier(
        random_state=42,
        max_iter=NN_MAX_ITER,
        early_stopping=True,
        validation_fraction=0.12,
        n_iter_no_change=20,
        tol=1e-4,
    )
    nn_pipe = Pipeline([("scaler", StandardScaler()), ("mlp", _nn_base)])
    _nn_param_distributions = {
        "mlp__hidden_layer_sizes": [
            (64,),
            (128,),
            (256,),
            (32, 32),
            (64, 32),
            (128, 64),
            (256, 128),
            (128, 128),
        ],
        "mlp__alpha": loguniform(1e-7, 5e-2),
        "mlp__activation": ["relu", "tanh"],
        "mlp__solver": ["adam"],
        "mlp__learning_rate_init": loguniform(5e-5, 2e-2),
        "mlp__batch_size": [32, 64, 128, 256],
    }
    nn_search = RandomizedSearchCV(
        nn_pipe,
        param_distributions=_nn_param_distributions,
        n_iter=min(NN_RANDOM_TRIALS, 80),
        scoring="average_precision",
        cv=StratifiedKFold(n_splits=NN_SEARCH_CV, shuffle=True, random_state=42),
        n_jobs=-1,
        random_state=42,
        refit=True,
        verbose=0,
    )
    nn_search.fit(X_fit, y_fit)
    print("§7b NN best CV AP:", round(float(nn_search.best_score_), 5))
    print("§7b NN best params:", nn_search.best_params_)
    best_nn_merge = clone(nn_search.best_estimator_)
    best_nn_merge.fit(X_merge, y_merge)
    NN_BLEND_AVAILABLE = True
    print("§7b NN refit on X_merge OK | features:", getattr(best_nn_merge, "n_features_in_", "?"))
```

```python
def _blend_extra_row(variant, p_c, p_te, w_cat, w_xgb, w_rf, w_tabpfn, w_nn=np.nan, w_lgb=np.nan, w_lr=np.nan):
    """Same metric columns as section 9 blend_table (needs t_grid_blend, best_threshold_f1_on_cal, etc. from section 9)."""
    t_opt = best_threshold_f1_on_cal(y_cal, p_c, t_grid_blend)
    row = {
        "variant": variant,
        "w_cat": w_cat,
        "w_xgb": w_xgb,
        "w_rf": w_rf,
        "w_tabpfn": w_tabpfn,
        "w_nn": w_nn,
        "w_lgb": w_lgb,
        "w_lr": w_lr,
        "pr_auc_cal": average_precision_score(y_cal, p_c),
        "max_f2_cal": max_f2_over_thresholds(y_cal, p_c, t_grid_blend),
        "pr_auc_test": average_precision_score(y_test, p_te),
        "roc_auc_test": roc_auc_score(y_test, p_te),
    }
    row.update(test_metrics_at_threshold(y_test, p_te, t_opt))
    return row
```

### ASSISTANT
Paste this **after** the base models are fitted (`best_cat`, `best_xgb`, `best_rf_merge`, `best_lgb`, `best_lr`) and **before** §9 ensemble. It evaluates each **raw** estimator on **`X_test` / `y_test`** at **threshold 0.5** (plus threshold-free PR-AUC / ROC-AUC). Optional **`best_nn_merge`** is included only if it exists.

```python
import numpy as np
from sklearn.metrics import (
    accuracy_score,
    average_precision_score,
    balanced_accuracy_score,
    classification_report,
    confusion_matrix,
    f1_score,
    precision_score,
    recall_score,
    roc_auc_score,
)

def _metrics_block(name, y_true, p_proba, threshold=0.5):
    y_true = np.asarray(y_true).astype(int).ravel()
    p = np.asarray(p_proba, dtype=float).ravel()
    y_hat = (p >= threshold).astype(int)
    print("\n" + "=" * 72)
    print(name, "| threshold =", threshold)
    print("=" * 72)
    print("Confusion matrix [[TN FP]\n                     [FN TP]]:")
    print(confusion_matrix(y_true, y_hat, labels=[0, 1]))
    print(
        "PR-AUC:", round(float(average_precision_score(y_true, p)), 4),
        "| ROC-AUC:", round(float(roc_auc_score(y_true, p)), 4),
    )
    print(
        "Accuracy:", round(float(accuracy_score(y_true, y_hat)), 4),
        "| Balanced acc:", round(float(balanced_accuracy_score(y_true, y_hat)), 4),
    )
    print(
        "Precision:", round(float(precision_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0)), 4),
        "| Recall:", round(float(recall_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0)), 4),
        "| F1:", round(float(f1_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0)), 4),
    )
    print("classification_report (0=neg, 1=pos):")
    print(classification_report(y_true, y_hat, digits=4, zero_division=0))

def _safe_predict_proba_pos(model, X):
    p = model.predict_proba(X)[:, 1]
    return np.asarray(p, dtype=float).ravel()

_candidates = [
    ("LR (grid best)", "best_lr"),
    ("RF grid → refit X_merge (best_rf_merge)", "best_rf_merge"),
    ("CatBoost (best, X_merge)", "best_cat"),
    ("XGBoost (best, X_merge)", "best_xgb"),
    ("LightGBM (best, X_merge)", "best_lgb"),
]

for label, key in _candidates:
    m = globals().get(key)
    if m is None:
        continue
    try:
        p_te = _safe_predict_proba_pos(m, X_test)
        _metrics_block(label, y_test, p_te, threshold=0.5)
    except Exception as e:
        print("\n", label, "skipped:", repr(e))

if globals().get("best_nn_merge") is not None:
    try:
        p_te = _safe_predict_proba_pos(best_nn_merge, X_test)
        _metrics_block("MLP pipeline (§7b best_nn_merge)", y_test, p_te, threshold=0.5)
    except Exception as e:
        print("\nNN skipped:", repr(e))

print("\nDone: per-model test metrics at t=0.5 (uncalibrated base estimators).")
```

If **`best_lgb`** was never created in your run, that row is skipped automatically. If you prefer **calibrated** models instead of raw `best_*`, swap names for `cal_cat`, `cal_xgb`, `cal_rf`, etc., but those belong **after** §8 and are partly used in ensembles.

### ASSISTANT
Here’s what was added and how to use it.

### New notebook: `code/modeling/fp_precision_mining.ipynb`

It:

1. **Loads** `data/result/modeling_advanced/fp_mining_bundle.joblib` (you create this once from `advanced.ipynb`; see below).
2. **Deploy winner FPs (test):** uses `p_cal_winner` / `p_test_winner` from the bundle, picks **F1-max threshold on `y_cal`** (grid `0.05…0.95`, step `0.01`, same idea as §9), then flags **test** FPs as `y_test == 0` and `p_test_winner >= t`. Writes  
   `.../fp_mining/false_positives_deploy_winner_test.csv`.
3. **TabPFN “max FP on cal negatives”:** on **`y_cal == 0`**, searches thresholds to **maximize** count of `p_tabpfn_cal >= t`, breaks ties with the **smallest** `t`, then applies that `t` on **test** and collects **test** FPs the same way. Writes  
   `.../fp_mining/false_positives_tabpfn_maxfp_cal_threshold_test.csv`.  
   If TabPFN was not in the bundle, this step is skipped and `fp_tab` stays all-false.
4. **Union:** writes `false_positives_union_test.csv` and `fp_mining_summary.json` (counts, thresholds, `win_name`).

Each CSV is **`X_test` rows** for FPs only, with `test_row_id` (row index in `X_test`), `y_true`, model probabilities, and `source`.

### One-time export from `advanced.ipynb` (after §9, with TabPFN in §8b if you want it)

Use the ready-made cell in:

`code/modeling/snippets/fp_mining_export_cell.py`

Paste that cell into `advanced.ipynb` after §9 has run and execute it. It writes:

`data/result/modeling_advanced/fp_mining_bundle.joblib`

Then open **`fp_precision_mining.ipynb`** and run all cells (from `code/modeling/` so paths `../../data/...` resolve like in `advanced.ipynb`).

**Note:** “Threshold with the most false positives” on calibration negatives is **degenerate** in the limit (very low `t` flags almost all negatives). The notebook uses a **dense grid** and the **smallest `t` among ties** for the maximum count, so you get the most aggressive threshold on that grid—expect **many** TabPFN FPs on test. You can tighten the grid or add a minimum `t` later if you want a smaller pool.

### ASSISTANT
Local `main` is **1 commit ahead** (“changes”) and **17 commits behind** `origin/main`. Git 2.27+ requires we choose **merge** or **rebase**.

**Option A — merge** (keeps your commit; adds a merge commit):

```bash
git pull --no-rebase origin main
```

**Option B — rebase** (replays your 1 commit on top of remote; linear history):

```bash
git pull --rebase origin main
```

**Optional default** so plain `git pull` works later:

```bash
git config pull.rebase false   # merge (default many teams use)
# or
git config pull.rebase true    # rebase by default
```

Use **rebase** if you want history like `… remote … then your “changes” on top`. Use **merge** if you prefer not to rewrite your existing commit.

I won’t run `git pull` here unless you say which you want (**merge** or **rebase**); if there are conflicts, Git will stop and list files to fix.

Here’s what changed in **`code/modeling/llm_tabular_small_n.ipynb`**:

### Llama only (Ollama)
- **`LLM_BACKEND` / OpenAI** removed from the flow: only **`predict_ollama`** is used.
- §1 installs **`httpx`** only (no `openai` package).
- Config sets **`OLLAMA_MODEL`** (default `llama3`) and **`OLLAMA_URL`**; **`REQUEST_SLEEP_S`** lowered to `0.05` for local calls.
- §5 metrics metadata records **`backend: "ollama"`** and **`model: OLLAMA_MODEL`**.

### Balanced train / test (50/50 per split)
- After cleaning, **`m_cap = min(n0, n1, MAX_LABELED_PATIENTS // 2)`** rows **per class** are sampled.
- **Train pool** and **test** each get **`floor(TRAIN_POOL_FRAC * m_cap)`** vs **`m_cap − that`** examples **per class**, so **equal 0/1 counts in both splits** (when `TRAIN_POOL_FRAC` leaves at least 1 per class in each).

### Readable inputs + your system prompt
- **`COLUMN_MAPPING`** is in the config cell (same keys as your CSV, including **`History of peripheral vascualr disease`**).
- After rename, the target is **`label`**; leakage column is dropped as **`time_since_stent_implantation_months`** (`DROP_AFTER_RENAME`).
- **`system_message`** is exactly the block you provided (VLST cardiology + JSON + **`classifications`**).
- Few-shot and query patients are sent as **`row_to_json_obj`**: one **JSON object per patient** with **sorted keys** for stable reading.
- Few-shot answers use **`{"classifications": [0_or_1]}`**; the parser accepts **`classifications`** (or legacy **`label`**) and optional **`p_event`** (defaults to **0/1** if missing for ROC/PR).

### Docs
- Intro and §3 markdown updated for **Llama/Ollama**, **balancing**, and **renamed columns**.

**Run:** start **Ollama** with your model (e.g. `ollama run llama3`), then run the notebook from the repo venv. Override with **`OLLAMA_MODEL`** / **`OLLAMA_URL`** env vars if needed.

### USER
<user_query>
I ran this into notebook terminal is it right or I should have done it else where?
ollama run llama3
</user_query>

### ASSISTANT
Here’s the distinction:

### What `ollama run llama3` does
It starts an **interactive chat** in that terminal (and may **pull** the model if it’s missing). That’s fine for **checking** that Llama works and that the model is installed.

### What the notebook needs
The notebook calls **`http://127.0.0.1:11434/api/chat`**. That only needs the **Ollama service** listening on 11434 (usually started automatically when Ollama is installed / the app is running). It does **not** require you to keep `ollama run llama3` open in any particular terminal.

### So: notebook terminal vs elsewhere?
- **Not wrong** to run `ollama run llama3` in the **notebook terminal** — same as any other terminal.
- Often **more convenient in a normal system terminal** so the Jupyter terminal isn’t stuck in the chat UI while you work.
- You **don’t** need that interactive session **running while** you execute cells; you can exit it (`/bye` or Ctrl+D) after you’ve confirmed the model loads.

### One-time setup (typical)
```bash
ollama pull llama3
```
Then run the notebook; if requests fail, start or restart the Ollama app / `ollama serve` and try again.

**Summary:** Running it in the notebook terminal is okay; for day-to-day use, a separate terminal is nicer, and you only need the **service + pulled model**, not a permanent `ollama run` session, for the notebook API.

### ASSISTANT
Here’s what usually explains a “stuck” first step in that notebook.

### If you mean the **first code cell** (§1 — `pip install httpx`)
That cell runs **`subprocess.check_call([..., "pip", "install", ...])`**. It can look frozen because **`pip` often prints nothing** until the download finishes.

**Common causes**
- **Slow or blocked network** (first-time download of `httpx` / wheels).
- **Another `pip` process** holding the lock (another notebook, terminal, or IDE updating packages).
- **Very slow disk** or antivirus scanning new files.

**What to do**
1. **Wait 1–3 minutes** once; if nothing happens, use **Kernel → Interrupt** and try again.
2. In a normal terminal (with the **same venv** the notebook uses), run once:
   ```bash
   python -m pip install "httpx>=0.27"
   ```
   Then **comment out or skip** the pip cell in the notebook so it doesn’t run every time.
3. Confirm the notebook kernel is your **project `.venv`**, not a bare system Python (the cell raises if it thinks you’re not in a venv).

### If you mean **`In [1]`** is the **config** cell (imports + `DATA_ROOT`)
That cell should finish in seconds unless **`pd.read_csv` / path logic** is wrong — but your config cell doesn’t read CSV; it only resolves paths. So “stuck” there is uncommon unless the kernel is **still busy** from a previous run (e.g. pip still running).

### If you mean a **later** cell (Ollama / LLM)
That’s different: long **HTTP timeouts** (we use **300s**), many **test rows × `REQUEST_SLEEP_S`**, or **Ollama loading a big model** on first request. That’s not “cell 1” in the notebook file order, but it can be the first *slow* cell you notice.

**Quick check:** When it’s “stuck”, is the **kernel busy indicator** (solid circle) on? If yes, it’s still working or waiting on **network/subprocess/Ollama**. If the circle is idle but the cell shows `[*]`, the UI can be out of sync — try **restart kernel** and run again.

If you tell me **exactly which cell title** shows the `[*]` (e.g. “Optional: install HTTP client” vs “Configuration”), I can narrow it to one cause.

### ASSISTANT
Use any of these in the **same environment** as your notebook (same terminal or the venv’s `python`).

### 1. Show installed version (best)
```bash
python -m pip show httpx
```
If it’s installed you’ll see **`Version:`** (e.g. `0.27.0`). If not: *WARNING: Package(s) not found*.

### 2. Check from Python
```bash
python -c "import httpx; print(httpx.__version__)"
```
Works only if `httpx` is importable; prints the version.

### 3. See if it satisfies `>=0.27`
```bash
python -m pip install "httpx>=0.27" --dry-run
```
If already satisfied, pip says something like **“Requirement already satisfied”** and won’t install.

In **Jupyter**, one cell:
```python
import importlib.util
spec = importlib.util.find_spec("httpx")
print("httpx installed:", spec is not None)
if spec:
    import httpx
    print("version:", httpx.__version__)
```

### ASSISTANT
Here’s what that error means and what we changed.

### What `ReadTimeout` is
**Not** “cell 4” in the sense of the short **Configuration** cell. The traceback is from the **Ollama / LLM cell** (`predict_ollama` → `client.post(...)`): **httpx** opened a connection to Ollama but **gave up waiting for the full HTTP response** before the **read** timeout.

Typical reasons:

1. **First request** while the model is **loading** (large weights, CPU or limited GPU).
2. **Slow hardware** — full `llama3` on CPU can take **many minutes** per patient with a **big prompt** (few-shot + wide JSON).
3. Your code used **`timeout=300.0`** → **5 minutes** max; Ollama didn’t finish in time → **`ReadTimeout`**.

### What to do
1. **Warm up Ollama once** (separate terminal): `ollama run llama3` → ask “hi” → quit, so the model is loaded before the notebook loop.
2. **Use a smaller / faster model** if needed, e.g. `OLLAMA_MODEL=llama3.2:1b` (set in env or in the config cell).
3. **Raise the timeout** — the notebook is updated to:
   - **`OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_S`** (default **1200** seconds, overridable with env `OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_S`).
   - Explicit **`httpx.Timeout(..., read=OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_S, ...)`** so the **read** phase isn’t stuck at an implicit shorter limit.
   - **`num_predict`: 256** in Ollama options so the model **stops generating sooner** (you only need a short JSON).

Re-run **§2 Configuration**, then the **Ollama** cell. Optionally:

```bash
export OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_S=2400
```

before starting Jupyter if runs are still slow.

**Summary:** Nothing is “wrong” with your CSV or config cell; Ollama simply **didn’t return within the old 300s read window**. Longer timeout + smaller model + warm-up usually fixes it.

### ASSISTANT
Here’s what was going wrong and what we added.

### Why you still see timeouts after “many minutes”
`ReadTimeout` means **Ollama did not finish the HTTP response within the read timeout** (often **first row** = huge prompt + cold model, or **CPU-only** full `llama3`). Raising the limit helps, but you still need to see **whether the server answers at all** and **how long a tiny call takes**.

### What we changed in `llm_tabular_small_n.ipynb`

1. **New §3b “Ollama preflight”** (run **after** config + data load, **before** the big §4 loop):
   - **GET** `…/api/tags` — confirms Ollama is up and lists models (quick timeout).
   - **Minimal POST** to the same **`/api/chat`** URL: system + user “Say exactly: PONG”, `num_predict=16`, and prints **`elapsed_s`** for that round-trip.
   - Catches **`httpx.ReadTimeout`** and prints a short hint (model / timeout / `OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_S`).
   - Includes **`import httpx`** in that cell so it runs standalone.

2. **Bugfix** in preflight: invalid `_txt[:120]!r` in `print` → **`repr(_txt[:120])`**.

3. **§4 main loop**: logs **per-row time** for the **first** test row and roughly **every 20%** of rows, plus cumulative seconds, so you can see if the first patient dominates time.

### What you should do
1. Restart kernel → run through **§2**, **§3**, then **§3b preflight**.
2. If **Preflight 1** fails → Ollama not running / wrong host (`ollama serve` or Ollama app).
3. If **Preflight 1** OK but **Preflight 2** times out → model too heavy or not pulled; try e.g. `OLLAMA_MODEL=llama3.2:1b` or raise `export OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_S=3600`.
4. Only if **both** preflight checks pass, run the **full few-shot** cell.

If you paste **Preflight 1 + 2** stdout (or the exact error line), we can narrow it further (wrong model name, RAM, etc.).

OK | elapsed_s 6.12 | raw_preview: 'Table'

### ASSISTANT
Preflight passing only shows that **Ollama is up** and that a **tiny** chat completes in ~6s. That does **not** contradict an earlier **`ReadTimeout`** on the **full** pipeline.

### Why the big cell could still hit `ReadTimeout` (before fixes)

1. **Completely different request size**  
   Preflight: **2 short messages** + **`num_predict`: 16** → small job, ~6s.  

   Full §4 loop: **12 few-shot patients** (each a **full JSON** of **all** columns) + **system** + **each test patient** (again full JSON). That can be **orders of magnitude more tokens** than the probe. On CPU (or even GPU), **one** such call can easily run **many minutes** → with a **300s** read cap you get **`ReadTimeout`**.

2. **You used to cap reads at 300s**  
   Anything slower than 5 minutes per HTTP response failed, even if Ollama was “healthy.” We later raised **`OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_S`** and set explicit **`httpx.Timeout(read=...)`** plus **`num_predict`** on the real call to limit endless generation.

3. **Cold start vs warm**  
   The first heavy request can pay **extra** setup (weights already loaded after preflight helps, but **KV cache / long context** still costs a lot).

4. **`'Table'` instead of `PONG`**  
   That’s just the model **ignoring** the tiny instruction; it doesn’t affect connectivity. It shows the **small** request returned quickly anyway.

### Practical takeaway

- **Timeout** = “this **single HTTP read** took longer than allowed,” not “Ollama is down.”  
- **Preflight** = “Ollama answers **small** posts.”  
- **Full notebook** = “each row is a **huge** prompt” → **much** slower; fix is **longer timeout**, **smaller prompts** (fewer shots / fewer columns / shorter JSON), and/or **smaller model** — not only “is Ollama running?”

The new loop **logging** (`this_row_s`, `cum_s`) is there so you can confirm: if **row 1** already shows hundreds of seconds, it’s **prompt size / hardware**, not a mystery disconnect.

### ASSISTANT
Here is a concise take and what was changed in the notebook.

## What that log means

Ollama is fitting your **full prompt** (system + few-shot + query) into **`num_ctx`**. With **`limit=4096`** and **`prompt≈10092`**, most of the beginning of the prompt is thrown away (`keep=25` keeps a tiny prefix). The model then sees a **mangled** context, so results are unreliable and runs can feel slow or weird. This is separate from HTTP timeouts.

## Recommendations (models vs. prompt)

1. **Prefer shrinking the prompt first** (cheapest, works on any GPU). Your VLST table has **many columns**; only a subset are in `COLUMN_MAPPING`, but the notebook was still sending **every** feature column as JSON, which explodes token count. **Mapped-only features** are usually enough for this experiment and align with the “readable names” story.

2. **Optional: raise `num_ctx`** via the API **if** you have RAM/VRAM headroom. That does **not** require switching to 70B. `export OLLAMA_NUM_CTX=8192` (or `16384`) on the same `llama3:8b` tag often works if the machine can hold the larger KV cache.

3. **Bigger models (70B / 72B)** mainly help **quality**, not context length. They are heavy; only use them if you actually want that tradeoff **and** your hardware can run them.

4. **`llama3.1:8b` with a large context** is reasonable **if** you truly need a **very** wide prompt and have the memory; pull the tag Ollama documents and still set `num_ctx` appropriately.

## Changes applied in `llm_tabular_small_n.ipynb`

- **`LLM_USE_MAPPED_COLUMNS_ONLY = True`** (default): after the leakage drop, **`feature_df` is restricted to columns that appear in `COLUMN_MAPPING`** (excluding the label and dropped columns). That cuts JSON size dramatically and should remove the 4096 truncation for typical few-shot sizes.
- **`OLLAMA_NUM_CTX`**: if set in the environment (e.g. `export OLLAMA_NUM_CTX=8192`), it is passed in **`options.num_ctx`** for both the **preflight** chat and **`predict_ollama`**.
- **Config print** and **`llm_small_n_meta.json`** now record **`num_ctx`**, **`llm_use_mapped_columns_only`**, and **`n_llm_feature_columns`**.

If you need **all** raw CSV columns in the prompt again, set **`LLM_USE_MAPPED_COLUMNS_ONLY = False`** in §2 and combine with a **larger** model context (`OLLAMA_NUM_CTX` and/or a long-context tag), accepting memory cost.

Here is what was wrong and what we changed.

## Root cause

Your config showed **`num_ctx=None`**, so **nothing was sent** to Ollama for the context window. Ollama then kept the built‑in **4096** limit while your prompt was ~**5092** tokens → truncation.

## Fixes applied in `llm_tabular_small_n.ipynb`

1. **Always pass `num_ctx`**  
   Default **`OLLAMA_NUM_CTX = 8192`** in §2 if the env var is unset. Preflight and `predict_ollama` both set **`"num_ctx": OLLAMA_NUM_CTX`** on every request (no optional branch).

2. **Smaller prompts**  
   - **`FEW_SHOT_PER_CLASS = 3`** (was 6 → fewer few-shot tokens).  
   - **Compact JSON** (`separators=(",", ":")`).  
   - **`MAX_JSON_VALUE_CHARS = 80`** to trim long cell strings when serializing.

3. **Diagnostics**  
   After building the few-shot block, the notebook prints an **approximate token count** for the first test row and **warns** if it is still &gt; ~**88%** of `num_ctx` (tune `FEW_SHOT_PER_CLASS`, `OLLAMA_NUM_CTX`, or `MAX_JSON_VALUE_CHARS`).

4. **Colab (§0)**  
   - New **§0** markdown + code: on **Google Colab** it installs Ollama, starts **`ollama serve`**, sets defaults **`OLLAMA_MODEL=llama3.1:8b`** and **`OLLAMA_NUM_CTX=8192`**, and runs **`ollama pull`**.  
   - **§1** pip cell: **no venv** on Colab; same venv check as before locally.  
   - **`VLST_REPO_ROOT`** env (checked first in `_data_root()`) for a Drive layout like `.../vlst/data/raw/VLST.csv`.  
   - Intro + §1 text updated for Colab order.

5. **Removed** the trailing empty code cell.  
6. **Meta JSON** now includes **`max_json_value_chars`**.

If you need more headroom on a big GPU, set in the environment or in the notebook, e.g. **`OLLAMA_NUM_CTX=16384`** (uses more VRAM).

---

## Can you use Colab for a heavier, longer-context model?

**Yes, with caveats:**

- Colab gives you a **GPU** (often **T4 ~15 GB**) so **8B** models with **`num_ctx` 8k–16k** are realistic; **70B** is usually **not** practical on free tiers.
- Sessions **reset** when they disconnect; you’ll need to **re-run §0** and often **`ollama pull`** again.
- **Free tier** has **quotas and limits**; long runs can be cut off.
- For **very** long context, prefer something like **`llama3.1:8b`** and a **modest** `num_ctx` you can actually load, instead of a huge model that does not fit.

**Typical Colab flow:** **Runtime → GPU** → run **§0** → put `VLST.csv` on Drive (or upload) → set **`VLST_REPO_ROOT`** or **`MANUAL_VLST_ROOT`** in §2 → run the rest.

Re-run **§2 (config)** so you see something like **`num_ctx=8192`**, then **§4**; the approximate prompt line should sit **below** 8192 and Ollama should stop logging input truncation for that run. If you still see warnings, lower **`FEW_SHOT_PER_CLASS`** to **2** or raise **`OLLAMA_NUM_CTX`**.

### ASSISTANT
Here is the direct answer:

## Colab vs laptop — where is Ollama?

When you open the notebook **in Google Colab**, the Python kernel runs on **Google’s VM** (in their datacenter), **not** on your laptop. Your laptop is only running the **browser**.

So:

- **Colab:** Ollama must run **on that Colab VM**. That is why §0 installs Ollama there and starts **`ollama serve`** in that same session. Inference happens **next to** the notebook process (often with the Colab GPU).
- **Local Jupyter / Cursor:** The kernel runs **on your machine**, so Ollama must run **on your laptop** (Ollama Desktop or `ollama serve` in a terminal).

You do **not** use “your laptop’s Ollama” from inside Colab unless you expose it over the network and change **`OLLAMA_URL`** — that is uncommon and not what the notebook is set up for.

## Why `localhost` / `127.0.0.1`?

**`127.0.0.1`** always means **“this computer” from the point of view of the code that is running.**

| Where the notebook runs | What “localhost” refers to |
|---------------------------|----------------------------|
| Colab                     | The **Colab VM**           |
| Your laptop               | **Your laptop**            |

So **`http://127.0.0.1:11434`** is correct for **`httpx`** in that notebook: it calls the Ollama API **on the same machine as the Python process**. No extra routing through your home PC.

## Where to run `ollama serve`?

- **Colab:** On the **Colab runtime** — either via the §0 cell (background process) or an equivalent `!ollama serve &` in that environment.
- **Laptop:** On **your machine**, before or while you run the notebook locally.

I added this explanation as a short paragraph in **§0** of `llm_tabular_small_n.ipynb` so it stays next to the Colab setup.

## What that error is

`CalledProcessError` means the shell command

`curl -fsSL https://ollama.com/install.sh | sh`

**exited with status 1** on the Colab VM. The notebook was using `check=True`, so Python turned that into an exception.

On **Colab**, that often happens because:

1. **Newer Ollama Linux builds** are shipped as **`.tar.zst`**, and the installer expects the **`zstd`** CLI. If `zstd` isn’t installed, the script exits with an error (often mentioning `zstd`).
2. Less often: **curl**, **SSL certs**, or **disk** issues during download/unpack.

So the failure is **on Google’s Colab machine**, not on your laptop—your browser only shows the traceback.

## What we changed

§0 is updated to:

1. Run **`apt-get install`** for **`zstd`**, **`curl`**, and **`ca-certificates`** before calling **`install.sh`**.
2. Run **`install.sh`** without aborting the cell immediately, and **print the last part of stdout/stderr** if it fails.
3. If there is still no `ollama` in **`PATH`**, **fall back** to downloading  
   `https://ollama.com/download/ollama-linux-<amd64|arm64>.tgz`  
   and **`tar -xzf` into `/usr/local`**, then fix **`PATH`** if needed.
4. If all of that fails, raise a clear error with a **one-line manual** `curl` + `tar` you can paste in a Colab cell.

There is also a short note in the §0 markdown about this `CalledProcessError` / `zstd` case.

Re-run the **§0** code cell on a **new** Colab runtime; the first run may take a few minutes (apt + install + pull). If anything still errors, the printed **`install.sh` stderr** (or the new RuntimeError) will be the next thing to check.

Here’s a concise summary of **what goes to Ollama** and what was changed in the notebook.

## What the notebook sends to Ollama (each test call)

For **every** scoring request to `POST /api/chat` the payload includes:

1. **`system`** — The fixed cardiology / VLST instruction and the rule that the model must answer with a JSON object containing `"classifications": [...]`.

2. **`user`** —  
   - Short task text (few-shot description, then the test patient).  
   - **Few-shot block**: `FEW_SHOT_PER_CLASS` examples from **label 0** and the same from **label 1** (drawn from the **train pool** only), each = `Patient JSON:` + one **JSON object of that row** + the line `{"classifications": [0 or 1]}`.  
   - **Query**: the **held-out test** row as one more `Patient JSON:` + JSON object.  

3. **What’s inside each “patient” JSON** — All **feature** columns after §3:  
   - Columns renamed via **`COLUMN_MAPPING`** where they exist.  
   - **Plus** any columns **not** in the map, still under their **original CSV names**.  
   - **Not** sent: target **`label`**, **`NO.`**, **`Name`**, and **`time_since_stent_implantation_months`** (leakage drop).  
   - Values are strings; very long strings may be cut at **`MAX_JSON_VALUE_CHARS`**.

So the model **never** receives the split identity—only **tabular fields** as JSON (plus your instructions).

---

## Changes applied (your requests)

| Setting | Now |
|--------|-----|
| **Cohort size** | **`MAX_LABELED_PATIENTS = 500`** (still capped by how many 0/1 rows exist in the CSV). |
| **Balance** | Unchanged logic: same number of **0** and **1** in the pool; **within each class**, `TRAIN_POOL_FRAC` of that class → train pool, the rest → test, so **train and test are each 50/50**; **positives (label 1)** are **equal in count to negatives in each split**, with **train vs test** counts given by **`n_tr_per_class`** and **`n_te_per_class`**. The load cell prints an extra **split balance** line. |
| **All columns** | **`LLM_USE_MAPPED_COLUMNS_ONLY = False`** — mapped names + all unmapped columns. |
| **Context** | Default **`num_ctx` 32768** in §2; Colab §0 also sets default **`OLLAMA_NUM_CTX` to 32768** if unset. |
| **Stronger default model** | **`OLLAMA_MODEL` default = `llama3.1:8b`**. You can still do e.g. `export OLLAMA_MODEL=qwen2.5:14b` after `ollama pull qwen2.5:14b`. |
| **Few-shot** | **`FEW_SHOT_PER_CLASS = 2`** to keep prompts smaller with **full** tables (4 few-shot patients total). Increase in §2 if you have **VRAM** and headroom in **`OLLAMA_NUM_CTX`**. |
| **Docs** | **§4** now describes the payload; **metadata** includes **`max_labeled_patients_cap`**. |

**Note:** If the dataset has **fewer** than 250 cases per class, `m_per_class` (and thus the 500 cap) will be **lower** than 500. 

**Note:** Scoring **~hundreds** of test rows with a **full** table and a **32k** context is **slow** and **heavy** on GPU RAM; if you OOM, set e.g. `export OLLAMA_NUM_CTX=16384` or turn **`LLM_USE_MAPPED_COLUMNS_ONLY`** back to `True` for a slimmer JSON.

Here is what changed and how columns work.

## Column names: no double sending

`df.rename(columns=_rename)` **replaces** the old header with the new one. A column is **either**:

- the **mapped** name (e.g. `Age` → `age`), **or**  
- an **unmapped** name (unchanged),

never both. The JSON is built from `feature_df` **column names** after that single rename, so you do **not** get the same field as both `age` and `Age`.

(If the CSV ever had two columns that rename to the same target, pandas would break; your `COLUMN_MAPPING` is one-to-one for the defined keys.)

## Split: all negatives, all positives, “balanced” in the stratified sense

The old logic **subsampled** to `min(positives, negatives, cap)` and forced **50/50** in the pool.

**Now:**

- **Every** row with label **0** and **every** row with label **1** is kept (after drops for missing label / leakage).
- **Train / test** is **stratified by class**:
  - class **0**: `floor(TRAIN_POOL_FRAC × n₀)` → train, the rest → test  
  - class **1**: `floor(TRAIN_POOL_FRAC × n₁)` → train, the rest → test  
- So with **~5000** negatives and **~92** positives and `TRAIN_POOL_FRAC = 0.75` you get roughly:
  - train: **~3750** neg, **~69** pos  
  - test: **~1250** neg, **~23** pos  

**Prevalence** (~1.8% positive) is **the same in train and test** (up to `floor` rounding), not 50/50 class balance.

`MAX_LABELED_PATIENTS` is **removed**. Metadata now includes **`train_pool_frac`** instead of a labeled cap.

## Docs / code updates

- **Intro** and **§3** describe the full-cohort stratified split and “one name per column.”
- **§4** text updated so it no longer claims 50/50 splits.
- **Load cell** prints full cohort counts, train/test counts, % positive, and per-class train/test **n**.

**Practical note:** With **~1250** test rows and **all columns**, §4 will issue **~1250** Ollama calls; that can take a long time. If you want fewer calls, we can add an optional **test subsample** or **max test size** (say only for evaluation) in a follow-up.

### ASSISTANT
Here’s what was wrong before and what the notebook does **now**.

## What you asked for

- **Exactly `COHORT_TARGET_N` rows (default 500)** in the analysis cohort — not the full sheet.
- You **cannot** put “all ~5000 negatives” into 500 rows; the notebook **samples** negatives from the pool **without replacement** until the cohort is full.
- **Negatives (and positives) are stratified**: the same **`TRAIN_POOL_FRAC`** is applied **within class 0** and **within class 1**, so **negative** counts match that ratio in train vs test (same idea for positives).

## Implemented logic (`§2` + load cell)

1. **`COHORT_TARGET_N = 500`** in configuration (you can change it).
2. If **`#positives < 500`**: take **every positive**, then draw **`500 − (# positives)`** negatives at random from all negatives (no duplicate rows).
3. If **`#positives ≥ 500`** (unusual here): the cohort is a **random sample of 500 positives only** (no negatives in that cohort) — the code raises a clear situation; for VLST with ~92 positives this path does not run.
3. On that **500-row cohort**, **shuffle within class 0** and **within class 1**, then split with **`TRAIN_POOL_FRAC`** (e.g. 75% / 25%) **separately for negatives and positives**.

So with **92 positives** and **`TRAIN_POOL_FRAC = 0.75`** you get about:

- Cohort: **408 negatives + 92 positives = 500**
- Train: **~306 neg, ~69 pos**  
- Test: **~102 neg, ~23 pos**  

(integers come from `floor`, so counts may differ by 1.)

Docs (**intro**, **§3**, **§4**) and **metrics** (`cohort_target_n`) are updated to match.

Here is what was implemented.

### Why `ensemble_blend_comparison.csv` alone is not enough
That file has **metrics** (`pr_auc_cal`, etc.) per blend variant, but **not** the per-row **probabilities** on cal/test. False positives need **`p_test_winner`**, so they must come from the same export as before (or the new NPZ).

### 1. `code/modeling/snippets/fp_mining_export_cell.py`
After writing `fp_mining_bundle.joblib` it also writes:
- **`deploy_winner_arrays.npz`** — `p_cal`, `p_test`, `X_test`, `y_test`, `y_cal`, and TabPFN probs if available  
- **`deploy_winner_meta.json`** — `win_name`, `ENSEMBLE_DEPLOY`, `feature_names`, `TABPFN_BLEND_AVAILABLE`, **`pool_variant_keys`** (same order as `pool_pr_cal` in §9)

Re-run this snippet in the **advanced** kernel after §9 so these files exist next to your results.

### 2. `code/modeling/fp_precision_mining.ipynb` §1
- Loads **`fp_mining_bundle.joblib`** if present, otherwise **`deploy_winner_arrays.npz` + `deploy_winner_meta.json`**.
- Prints **`win_name`** and **`ENSEMBLE_DEPLOY`** from that artifact (that **is** the §9 deploy winner).
- If **`ensemble_blend_comparison.csv`** exists and **`pool_variant_keys`** are known:
  - Restricts to the **same pool** as §9, takes **argmax `pr_auc_cal`** (ties reported).
  - Maps **`stacking_lr_cal` → `stacking_lr_on_probs_cal`** for the CSV row name and warns if the mapped variant is not among the top-PR tied rows.
  - If the bundle still has **`pool_pr_cal`**, checks calibration PR-AUC vs the CSV row (warns if &gt; 0.02 apart).

Sections **2–4** are unchanged in logic: they still compute deploy-winner FPs at F1-optimal **cal** threshold, optional TabPFN FPs, union CSV, and **`fp_mining_summary.json`**.

### What you should run
1. **`advanced.ipynb` through §9**  
2. Paste/run **`snippets/fp_mining_export_cell.py`** once in that kernel (creates/updates bundle + npz + meta).  
3. Run **`fp_precision_mining.ipynb`** top to bottom.

If you only have an **old** `fp_mining_bundle.joblib` (no npz), the FP notebook still works; re-running the export adds NPZ/meta and **`pool_variant_keys`** so CSV checks work fully.

**Yes, for the deployed stream.** Section 9 picks **`p_cal` / `p_test`** from **`pool_pr_cal`** using **`ENSEMBLE_DEPLOY`** (default **`max_pr_cal_pool`** → **`win_name`** = argmax **PR-AUC on calibration** over that pool). Sections 10–11 use those same probabilities; the export snippet saves **those** vectors as **`p_cal_winner` / `p_test_winner`**.

So FP mining is tied to **whatever deploy policy you used in §9**, not a different “best” from elsewhere.

To make that explicit in artifacts, **`fp_mining_export_cell.py`** now adds **`deploy_selection`** to the bundle and **`deploy_winner_meta.json`**:

- **`ENSEMBLE_DEPLOY`**
- **`winner_selection_rule`** (text for `max_pr_cal_pool` / `nocat_f2_cal` / `stacking_lr_cal`)
- **`win_name`**

**`fp_precision_mining.ipynb`** prints that JSON when present (re-run the export after pulling these changes so old bundles pick up the new field).

### Ten runs of `advanced` + FP mining without overwriting

```python
import os
```

Outputs go under **`OUT_DIR / <tag>`** (see **`VLST_FP_RUN_TAG`** in the intro cell).

1. Keeps the **legacy** “max FP on cal negatives” CSV: **`false_positives_tabpfn_maxfp_cal_threshold_test.csv`**

   - **`tabpfn_threshold_outputs.json`** — short manifest of those files  

**Union (§4)** is now **deploy-winner FPs** ∪ **TabPFN positives at any `t_grid` threshold**, which on negatives is the same as **`p_tabpfn_test ≥ min(t_grid)`** (0.05). The CSV adds **`fp_tabpfn_legacy_t`** vs **`fp_tabpfn_any_grid_t`**.

### Kaggle-friendly outputs for another notebook

In **`OUT_DIR`** you now also get:

- **`fp_mining_summary.json`** — includes **`deploy_selection`**, **`VLST_FP_RUN_TAG`**, paths summary, counts  
- **`kaggle_fp_outputs_manifest.json`** — **absolute paths** to every artifact under that run

### USER

I don't want to push all commits just the last one, can I?

### ASSISTANT
Here’s how Git behaves:

### You’re already only sending “what’s new”
`git push` (for your current branch) only sends **commits that exist on your branch but not on the remote**. It does **not** re-push your whole history every time. If you have **one** new commit on top of `origin/main`, only that commit (and any new objects it needs) gets uploaded.

### You can’t push “only the last commit” and hide older local commits
If you have **several** local commits that aren’t on the remote, a normal push will send **all of them**, because the remote needs the **chain** (parent → child). Git doesn’t offer “push commit #3 but not #1 and #2.”

### If you want exactly **one** commit on the remote for all your work
Squash your local work into one commit, then push:

```bash
git rebase -i origin/main   # or origin/<branch> — mark commits as "squash"
# resolve, then:
git push origin <branch>
```

(If you already pushed those commits before, fixing history needs **`git push --force-with-lease`** — only if you understand the risk for collaborators.)

### If you’re worried about pushing **other branches**
Push only this branch:

```bash
git push origin <branch-name>
```

Don’t use `git push --all` unless you intend to update every branch.

**Short answer:** One `git push` for your branch only uploads **unpushed** commits; if that’s a single local commit, you’re only adding that one. To avoid sending **multiple** local commits, squash them first or keep one commit ahead of `origin`.

### ASSISTANT
Here’s what was added and how it behaves.

### New notebook: `code/modeling/tabpfn_fp_followup.ipynb`

**Cohort (from `fp_precision_mining` outputs)**  
- Loads **`X_test` / `y_test`** from the same FP export as FP mining (`fp_mining_bundle.joblib` or NPZ+meta), using the same **input** path logic (env + Kaggle discovery).  
- Reads **`false_positives_union_test.csv`** from the FP-mining **output** directory (`VLST_FP_OUTPUT_DIR` or, on Kaggle, `/kaggle/working/vlst_fp_mining_output`, optionally + **`VLST_FP_RUN_TAG`** — same rules as `fp_precision_mining.ipynb`).  
- **Positives:** every test index with **`y_test == 1`**.  
- **Negatives:** exactly **`n_pos`** rows — first use negatives listed in the union CSV (false-positive **potential**), then sample **true negatives** (`y == 0` and **not** in that union set). If there are more union-negatives than positives, it randomly subsamples union-negatives (`VLST_TABPFN_FOLLOWUP_SEED`, default **42**).

**TabPFN**  
- **Train / validation / test:** 70% / 15% / 15%, stratified.  
- **`TABPFN_TOKEN`** required (same as `advanced` §8b). TabPFN settings aligned with `advanced` (e.g. `n_estimators=8`, `ignore_pretraining_limits=True`, device CUDA when available).

**Metrics**  
- For **test**: ROC-AUC, PR-AUC, accuracy, precision/recall/F1, `classification_report`, confusion matrix + plot — at  
  - fixed **0.5**,  
  - **t** that maximizes **F1 on validation** over **0.01…0.99**,  
  - **t** that maximizes **F0.5** (`fbeta_score(..., beta=0.5)`) on validation.  

“**Max F0**” is ambiguous in sklearn; the notebook treats **F0.5** as the usual “precision‑heavy” choice and states that explicitly. If you only care about **F1**, use the **F1-max** block.

**Paths on Kaggle:** set `VLST_FP_INPUT_ROOT` / `VLST_FP_OUTPUT_DIR` / `VLST_FP_RUN_TAG` if auto-discovery doesn’t match your layout.

You can open **`code/modeling/tabpfn_fp_followup.ipynb`** and run all cells after FP mining has produced both the bundle and **`false_positives_union_test.csv`**.

It does **not** include **`fp_mining_bundle.joblib`** (or **`deploy_winner_arrays.npz` + `deploy_winner_meta.json`**).

### What you still need (second source)

**`tabpfn_fp_followup.ipynb`** loads **`X_test` / `y_test` / features** from the **same export as FP mining** — i.e. the bundle produced by **`fp_mining_export_cell.py`** after **`advanced.ipynb` §9**:

- `fp_mining_bundle.joblib` **or**  
- `deploy_winner_arrays.npz` + `deploy_winner_meta.json`  
(and optionally `ensemble_blend_comparison.csv` for checks elsewhere)

So besides **`fp-output`**, you need either:

- **Another dataset** that contains that bundle (often named like your earlier **`vlst_fp_inputs`** folder), **or**  
- **One combined dataset** whose zip contains **both**:
  - the bundle folder, and  
  - `vlst_fp_mining_output/` with the CSVs you listed

| **Optional** | Extra copies of the same files if you split across datasets (then use env vars) |

### ASSISTANT
Kaggle is blocking you because you’re trying to **publish kernel output as a new dataset again** (same kernel → same “create dataset” flow). You **don’t need** a second upload for the bundle.

### What to do instead

1. **Do not** use “Create dataset from notebook output” again for the same `vlst_fp_inputs` files if you already published them once.
2. In **`tabpfn_fp_followup.ipynb`**, open **Add data** (right sidebar).
3. Under **Your datasets** (or wherever you saved it), **attach the dataset you already created** for `fp_precision_mining` — the one that already contains `fp_mining_bundle.joblib`, NPZ, meta, etc.
4. Check the **Inputs** sidebar for the **exact mount path** (often `/kaggle/input/<something>/vlst_fp_inputs/` if those files sit in a nested folder).
5. At the **top of the notebook**, before paths run, point the follow-up notebook at that folder:

```python
import os
os.environ["VLST_FP_INPUT_ROOT"] = "/kaggle/input/<mount_from_sidebar>/vlst_fp_inputs"  # adjust to match Inputs
```

Same for FP mining outputs if they’re a **second** dataset (`fp-output` / `vlst_fp_mining_output`):

```python
os.environ["VLST_FP_OUTPUT_DIR"] = "/kaggle/input/<mount>/vlst_fp_mining_output"  # or .../fp-output/...
```

After that, **`tabpfn_fp_followup.ipynb`** reads the bundle + union CSV **only from `/kaggle/input`** — no new dataset creation.

### Why this fixes both problems

| Problem | Fix |
|--------|-----|
| “Already have a dataset from this kernel” | Stop creating another dataset from the same output; **reuse** the existing dataset via **Add data**. |
| Avoid uploading the same files twice | One publish is enough; **every other notebook** only needs **Add data → same dataset**. |

### If you need a *different* name or a second copy

- **Option A (preferred):** One published dataset, many notebooks all **Add** it.
- **Option B:** Create a new dataset only from **different** content (e.g. zip on your machine) or a **new** kernel run, not a duplicate export of the same `vlst_fp_inputs` from the same run.

**TabPFN token** is still only the API secret, not a file dataset.

I can add a short “Kaggle: reuse existing dataset, set `VLST_FP_INPUT_ROOT` from sidebar” note to the first markdown cell of `tabpfn_fp_followup.ipynb` if you want that documented in-repo.

### ASSISTANT
### Why you saw `18 | 18 | 18 | 0`

The **old** logic built a **balanced** set: **one negative per positive** (`need = n_pos = 18`). It **filled negatives in order**: first every index from the **union (mined FP) pool**, and only if that pool had **fewer than 18** rows did it add **true negatives**.

You had **≥18** mined union negatives, so it took **18 of those only** → `union-negs used: 18`, `TN filled: 0`, total **36 = 18 pos + 18 neg**.

That does **not** match “all mined FPs + all positives + `n_pos` TNs”.

---

### What we changed (already in `tabpfn_fp_followup.ipynb`)

**§2 – “Load bundle + union CSV, build cohort”** (the code cell right under that heading) now does:

1. **`pos_idx`**: all test indices with **`y_test == 1`**.
2. **`fp_mined_neg`**: all **`y_test == 0`** indices whose **`test_row_id`** appears in **`false_positives_union_test.csv`** (your mined FP set).
3. **`tn_pool`**: **`y_test == 0`** and **`test_row_id` not in** that CSV (true negatives: not in the mined-FP set).
4. **`tn_sample_idx`**: **`n_pos`** indices drawn at random from **`tn_pool`** (same seed as before).

Then:

```text
idx = concat(pos_idx, fp_mined_neg, tn_sample_idx) → unique → X_cohort, y_cohort
```

So you get **all** positives, **all** mined-union negatives, and **exactly `n_pos` true negatives**. Total rows = **`n_pos + n_fp_mined + n_pos`**.

---

### Snippet (core of the new cohort block)

```python
gathered_ids = set(df_union["test_row_id"].astype(int).tolist())

fp_mined_neg = np.array(
    sorted({i for i in gathered_ids if 0 <= i < n and y_test[i] == 0}),
    dtype=int,
)
tn_pool = np.array(
    [i for i in range(n) if y_test[i] == 0 and i not in gathered_ids],
    dtype=int,
)

pos_idx = np.flatnonzero(y_test == 1)
n_pos = int(pos_idx.size)

tn_sample_idx = rng.choice(tn_pool, size=n_pos, replace=False)

idx = np.unique(np.concatenate([pos_idx, fp_mined_neg, tn_sample_idx]))
X_cohort = np.asarray(X_test[idx], dtype=np.float32)
y_cohort = np.asarray(y_test[idx], dtype=int)
```

---

### What **you** edit if you want a variant

- **TN policy**: change how **`tn_pool`** is built (line that builds the list comprehension `[i for i in range(n) if ...]`).
- **Mined-FP set**: change **`gathered_ids`** if you prefer a CSV other than **`false_positives_union_test.csv`** or extra filters.
- **Randomness**: `RANDOM_STATE` / `VLST_TABPFN_FOLLOWUP_SEED` at the top of §1.

The intro markdown cell at the top of the notebook was updated to describe this cohort design. Re-run from **§2** onward; class balance will now usually be **more negatives than positives** if the union FP list is long.

what is this legacy max-FP-on-cal-neg threshold.?
</user_query>

### ASSISTANT
It’s the old heuristic from `fp_precision_mining` section 3:

- Look only at **calibration negatives** (`y_cal == 0`)
- For many thresholds `t`, count how many rows satisfy `p_tabpfn_cal >= t`
- Pick the `t` that gives the **largest count** (ties → smallest `t`)
- Apply that chosen `t` on test to collect TabPFN FP rows

So “legacy max-FP-on-cal-neg threshold” = **threshold chosen to maximize number of predicted positives among calibration negatives** (not to optimize F1/F0.5/PR-AUC).

Why it’s called legacy:
- It intentionally pushes toward very permissive thresholds (often very low `t`)
- You now also have the stronger/all-threshold outputs (`tabpfn_fp_counts_by_threshold.csv`, long format, union-any-grid), so this single-threshold method is mostly kept for backward compatibility and comparison.

### USER
<timestamp>Wednesday, May 6, 2026, 11:52 AM (UTC+3:30)</timestamp>
<user_query>
what's the difference between calibration and test? would it be different from test negative?
</user_query>

### ASSISTANT
- **Calibration set (`X_cal`, `y_cal`)**: a held-out split used to tune probabilities/thresholds.  
- **Test set (`X_test`, `y_test`)**: a separate held-out split used only for final evaluation/reporting.

So yes, **calibration negatives** (`y_cal == 0`) are different rows from **test negatives** (`y_test == 0`) if the split is done correctly.

In your pipeline:
- legacy threshold picks `t` using **calibration negatives**
- then applies that `t` to **test negatives** to see what FPs you get on unseen data

That separation is important to avoid tuning directly on test.

### USER
<timestamp>Wednesday, May 6, 2026, 11:55 AM (UTC+3:30)</timestamp>
<user_query>
and why it has chosen calibration negative? and not train negatives?
</user_query>

### ASSISTANT
Because calibration is the **threshold-tuning split** in your pipeline.

- **Train negatives** are used to fit model parameters.
- If you also tune threshold on train negatives, you bias toward training data (optimistic overfit).
- **Calibration negatives** are held out from training, so they’re the right place to pick thresholds.
- **Test negatives** should stay untouched until final evaluation.

So the flow is:

1. train on train  
2. choose/tune threshold on calibration (including negatives there)  
3. report on test  

That’s why legacy “max-FP-on-cal-neg” uses `y_cal==0`, not train negatives.

```markdown
# TabPFN follow-up on full-data cohort + mined FPs (VLST)

Builds a cohort exactly as requested:

1. **All positives** from the original dataset (`VLST.csv`).
2. **True negatives sampled from original dataset** with count equal to positives.
3. **All mined false positives** from `false_positives_union_test.csv` (union export from `fp_precision_mining`).

Then splits this combined cohort into train/validation/test and fits TabPFN.

**Defaults**
- Full data path: `/kaggle/input/datasets/amirmahdidaraei/vlst-data/VLST.csv`
- FP outputs dir: `VLST_FP_OUTPUT_DIR` if set, else `/kaggle/working/vlst_fp_mining_output` on Kaggle.

**Env (optional)**
- `VLST_FULL_DATA_PATH` (override CSV path)
- `VLST_FULLDATA_TARGET_COL` (force target column)
- `VLST_FP_OUTPUT_DIR`, `VLST_FP_RUN_TAG`
- `TABPFN_TOKEN`
```

### Cell 1 (markdown)
```markdown
## 1. Paths and imports
```

### Cell 2 (code)
```python
import os
import warnings

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.metrics import (
    ConfusionMatrixDisplay,
    accuracy_score,
    average_precision_score,
    classification_report,
    confusion_matrix,
    f1_score,
    fbeta_score,
    precision_score,
    recall_score,
    roc_auc_score,
)
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split

warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", category=UserWarning)

RANDOM_STATE = int(os.environ.get("VLST_TABPFN_FOLLOWUP_SEED", "42"))

FULL_DATA_PATH = os.environ.get(
    "VLST_FULL_DATA_PATH",
    "/kaggle/input/datasets/amirmahdidaraei/vlst-data/VLST.csv",
)

def pick_fp_mining_output_dir() -> str:
    out = os.environ.get("VLST_FP_OUTPUT_DIR") or os.environ.get("VLST_FP_MINING_OUT")
    if out:
        out = os.path.expanduser(out.rstrip(os.sep))
    elif os.path.isdir("/kaggle/working"):
        out = "/kaggle/working/vlst_fp_mining_output"
    else:
        out = "../../data/result/modeling_advanced/fp_mining"
    tag = os.environ.get("VLST_FP_RUN_TAG", "").strip()
    if tag:
        out = os.path.join(out, tag)
    return out

FP_MINING_OUT = pick_fp_mining_output_dir()
UNION_CSV = os.path.join(FP_MINING_OUT, "false_positives_union_test.csv")

print("FULL_DATA_PATH:", FULL_DATA_PATH)
print("FP mining outputs (union CSV):", FP_MINING_OUT)
print("Union CSV:", UNION_CSV)
```

### Cell 3 (markdown)
```markdown
## 2. Load full dataset + union CSV, build requested cohort

Cohort composition:
- **All positives from full data** (`y == 1`).
- **True negatives from full data** (`y == 0`) sampled with count = number of positives.
- **All mined union FPs** from `false_positives_union_test.csv`.
```

### Cell 4 (code)
```python
if not os.path.isfile(FULL_DATA_PATH):
    raise FileNotFoundError(
        f"Missing full dataset at {FULL_DATA_PATH}. Set VLST_FULL_DATA_PATH before running."
    )
if not os.path.isfile(UNION_CSV):
    raise FileNotFoundError(
        f"Missing {UNION_CSV}. Run fp_precision_mining.ipynb first or set VLST_FP_OUTPUT_DIR / VLST_FP_RUN_TAG."
    )

df_full = pd.read_csv(FULL_DATA_PATH)
df_union = pd.read_csv(UNION_CSV)
if "test_row_id" not in df_union.columns:
    raise ValueError("Union CSV must contain test_row_id.")

# --- Target column detection on full dataset ---
forced_target = os.environ.get("VLST_FULLDATA_TARGET_COL", "").strip()
if forced_target:
    if forced_target not in df_full.columns:
        raise ValueError(f"VLST_FULLDATA_TARGET_COL={forced_target!r} not found in full dataset columns.")
    target_col = forced_target
else:
    preferred = [
        "target", "label", "y", "class", "Class", "Outcome", "outcome",
        "VLST", "vlst", "StentThrombosis", "stent_thrombosis",
    ]
    target_col = None
    for c in preferred:
        if c in df_full.columns:
            vals = set(pd.Series(df_full[c]).dropna().astype(int).unique().tolist())
            if vals.issubset({0, 1}) and len(vals) >= 1:
                target_col = c
                break
    if target_col is None:
        bin_cols = []
        for c in df_full.columns:
            s = pd.to_numeric(df_full[c], errors="coerce").dropna()
            vals = set(s.unique().tolist())
            if len(vals) > 0 and vals.issubset({0, 1}):
                bin_cols.append(c)
        if not bin_cols:
            raise ValueError(
                "Could not infer binary target column in full dataset. Set VLST_FULLDATA_TARGET_COL explicitly."
            )
        target_col = bin_cols[-1]

print("Using target column from full data:", target_col)

y_full = pd.to_numeric(df_full[target_col], errors="coerce").fillna(0).astype(int).to_numpy()
if not set(np.unique(y_full)).issubset({0, 1}):
    raise ValueError("Target column must be binary (0/1).")

# Use numeric feature columns (excluding target). Keep columns shared with union output.
X_full_df = df_full.drop(columns=[target_col]).copy()
for c in X_full_df.columns:
    X_full_df[c] = pd.to_numeric(X_full_df[c], errors="coerce")

# Union contains feature columns + metadata columns; shared numeric feature names are safest alignment.
meta_cols = {
    "test_row_id", "y_true", "p_winner", "p_tabpfn", "fp_winner",
    "fp_tabpfn_legacy_t", "fp_tabpfn_any_grid_t", "source", "threshold"
}
union_feature_candidates = [c for c in df_union.columns if c not in meta_cols]
shared_features = [c for c in union_feature_candidates if c in X_full_df.columns]
if not shared_features:
    raise ValueError(
        "No shared feature columns between full dataset and union CSV. "
        "Check preprocessing consistency or supply mapped features."
    )

X_full_use = X_full_df[shared_features].copy()
X_union_use = df_union[shared_features].copy()
for c in shared_features:
    X_union_use[c] = pd.to_numeric(X_union_use[c], errors="coerce")

# Median-impute numeric NaNs so TabPFN can train.
med = X_full_use.median(numeric_only=True)
X_full_use = X_full_use.fillna(med)
X_union_use = X_union_use.fillna(med)

pos_idx = np.flatnonzero(y_full == 1)
tn_pool_idx = np.flatnonzero(y_full == 0)
n_pos = int(pos_idx.size)
if n_pos == 0:
    raise ValueError("No positives in full dataset target column.")
if tn_pool_idx.size < n_pos:
    raise ValueError(f"Need {n_pos} true negatives but only {tn_pool_idx.size} available in full dataset.")

rng = np.random.RandomState(RANDOM_STATE)
tn_sample_idx = rng.choice(tn_pool_idx, size=n_pos, replace=False)

X_pos = X_full_use.iloc[pos_idx].to_numpy(dtype=np.float32)
X_tn = X_full_use.iloc[tn_sample_idx].to_numpy(dtype=np.float32)
X_fp = X_union_use.to_numpy(dtype=np.float32)

y_pos = np.ones(X_pos.shape[0], dtype=int)
y_tn = np.zeros(X_tn.shape[0], dtype=int)
y_fp = np.zeros(X_fp.shape[0], dtype=int)

X_cohort = np.vstack([X_pos, X_tn, X_fp])
y_cohort = np.concatenate([y_pos, y_tn, y_fp])

# Shuffle once before train/val/test split.
perm = rng.permutation(X_cohort.shape[0])
X_cohort = X_cohort[perm]
y_cohort = y_cohort[perm]

print(
    "Cohort | full-data positives:", X_pos.shape[0],
    "| full-data true negatives sampled:", X_tn.shape[0],
    "| mined union FPs added:", X_fp.shape[0],
    "| total rows:", X_cohort.shape[0],
)
print("Features used:", len(shared_features))
```

### Cell 5 (markdown)
```markdown
## 3. Train / validation / test split + TabPFN
```

### Cell 6 (code)
```python
X_tr, X_temp, y_tr, y_temp = train_test_split(
    X_cohort, y_cohort, test_size=0.3, stratify=y_cohort, random_state=RANDOM_STATE
)
X_val, X_te, y_val, y_te = train_test_split(
    X_temp, y_temp, test_size=0.5, stratify=y_temp, random_state=RANDOM_STATE
)

print("Train / val / test:", X_tr.shape[0], X_val.shape[0], X_te.shape[0])

token = os.environ.get("TABPFN_TOKEN")
if not token:
    raise RuntimeError(
        "Set TABPFN_TOKEN (shell export, Kaggle secret, or os.environ) — same as advanced.ipynb §8b."
    )

try:
    import torch
except ImportError:
    torch = None

TABPFN_N_ESTIMATORS = int(os.environ.get("TABPFN_N_ESTIMATORS", "8"))
TABPFN_DEVICE = os.environ.get("TABPFN_DEVICE")
if not TABPFN_DEVICE:
    TABPFN_DEVICE = "cuda" if torch is not None and torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"

from tabpfn import TabPFNClassifier

clf = TabPFNClassifier(
    random_state=RANDOM_STATE,
    n_estimators=TABPFN_N_ESTIMATORS,
    device=TABPFN_DEVICE,
    ignore_pretraining_limits=True,
    balance_probabilities=True,
)
clf.fit(X_tr, y_tr)

p_val = clf.predict_proba(X_val)[:, 1]
p_te = clf.predict_proba(X_te)[:, 1]

print("TabPFN fit OK | device:", TABPFN_DEVICE)
```

### Cell 7 (markdown)
```markdown
## 4. Thresholds (validation): max F1 and max F0.5 — metrics + confusion matrices on **test**
```

### Cell 8 (code)
```python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

t_grid = np.arange(0.01, 1.0, 0.01)

def best_threshold(y_true, p, grid, metric_fn):
    best_t, best_v = 0.5, -1.0
    for t in grid:
        y_hat = (p >= t).astype(int)
        v = float(metric_fn(y_true, y_hat))
        if v > best_v:
            best_v, best_t = v, float(t)
    return best_t, best_v

def f1m(y, yhat):
    return f1_score(y, yhat, zero_division=0)

def f05m(y, yhat):
    return fbeta_score(y, yhat, beta=0.5, zero_division=0)

t_f1, v_f1_val = best_threshold(y_val, p_val, t_grid, f1m)
t_f05, v_f05_val = best_threshold(y_val, p_val, t_grid, f05m)

print("Validation | best threshold for max F1:", round(t_f1, 4), "| F1 at that t:", round(v_f1_val, 4))
print(
    "Validation | best threshold for max F0.5 (fbeta, beta=0.5 — not 'F0'; use if you wanted precision tilt):",
    round(t_f05, 4),
    "| F0.5 at that t:",
    round(v_f05_val, 4),
)

y_hat_05 = (p_te >= 0.5).astype(int)
y_hat_f1 = (p_te >= t_f1).astype(int)
y_hat_f05 = (p_te >= t_f05).astype(int)

def report_block(name, y_true, y_hat, p):
    print("\n===", name, "===")
    print("ROC-AUC:", round(roc_auc_score(y_true, p), 4))
    print("PR-AUC:", round(average_precision_score(y_true, p), 4))
    print("Accuracy:", round(accuracy_score(y_true, y_hat), 4))
    print(
        "Precision / Recall / F1:",
        round(precision_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0), 4),
        round(recall_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0), 4),
        round(f1_score(y_true, y_hat, zero_division=0), 4),
    )
    print(classification_report(y_true, y_hat, digits=4))
    cm = confusion_matrix(y_true, y_hat)
    print("Confusion matrix [[TN FP] / [FN TP]]:\n", cm)
    plt.figure(figsize=(4, 3.5))
    ConfusionMatrixDisplay(cm).plot(cmap="Blues")
    plt.title(name)
    plt.tight_layout()
    plt.show()

report_block("Test @ threshold 0.5", y_te, y_hat_05, p_te)
report_block("Test @ t = F1-max on validation", y_te, y_hat_f1, p_te)
report_block("Test @ t = F0.5-max on validation", y_te, y_hat_f05, p_te)

summary = {
    "t_f1_max_val": float(t_f1),
    "t_f05_max_val": float(t_f05),
    "val_f1_at_t_f1": float(v_f1_val),
    "val_f05_at_t_f05": float(v_f05_val),
    "n_train": int(X_tr.shape[0]),
    "n_val": int(X_val.shape[0]),
    "n_test": int(X_te.shape[0]),
}
print("\nSummary:", summary)
```

### **Cell 0 (markdown)**
```markdown
# TabPFN follow-up on full-data cohort + mined FPs (VLST)

Builds a cohort exactly as requested:

1. **All positives** from the original dataset (`VLST.csv`).
2. **True negatives sampled from original dataset** with count equal to positives.
3. **All mined false positives** from `false_positives_union_test.csv` (union export from `fp_precision_mining`).

Then splits this combined cohort into train/validation/test and fits TabPFN.

**Defaults**
- Full data path: `/kaggle/input/datasets/amirmahdidaraei/vlst-data/VLST.csv`
- FP outputs dir: `VLST_FP_OUTPUT_DIR` if set, else `/kaggle/working/vlst_fp_mining_output` on Kaggle.

**Env (optional)**
- `VLST_FULL_DATA_PATH` (override CSV path)
- `VLST_FULLDATA_TARGET_COL` (force target column)
- `VLST_FP_OUTPUT_DIR`, `VLST_FP_RUN_TAG`
- `TABPFN_TOKEN`
```

---
